Global renormalized solutions for hard potential non-cutoff Boltzmann equation without defect measure Thanks: August 10, 2026
Yi-Long Luo, Jing-Xin Nie

Abstract
. The existence of global renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation with long-range interactions without angular cutoff was first established by Alexandre and Villani [Comm. Pure Appl. Math., 55(1), 30-70, 2002]. Their result relies on a definition of renormalized solutions involving a non-negative defect measure. In this paper, we address this issue for the inverse power law model in the case of hard potentials ( 0 ≤ γ ≤ 1 0\leq\gamma\leq 1 ). By exploiting the stronger coercivity estimates provided by hard potentials, we prove that the defect measure actually vanishes. Consequently, we establish the global existence of renormalized solutions for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation with hard potentials in the standard sense, without any defect measure. Finally, we construct a counterexample showing that the approach developed for the hard potential case fails for soft potential model ( − 3 < γ < 0 -3<\gamma<0 ). Keywords. Renormalized solutions, Existence, Boltzmann equation, Defect measure MSC2020. 76P05, 82C40, 35Q20, 45K05, 35A01



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Motivations

In 2002, Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
established the existence of renormalized solutions with a non-negative defect measure for the Boltzmann equation without angular cutoff in the whole space.
The aim of this paper is to prove that, for hard potentials, this defect measure vanishes.
The model considered in present work is given by the following Boltzmann equation

(1.1)

\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Q(f,f),

where the distribution function
f(t,x,v)
is non-negative. For any time
t\geq 0
,
f(t,\cdot,\cdot)
represents the particle density in phase space, with position
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
and velocity
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
. The term
Q(f,f)
denotes the Boltzmann collision operator, which acts only on the velocity dependence of the distribution function
f
(reflecting the physical assumption that collisions are localized in time and space),

(1.2)

Q(f,f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}dv_{*}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)(f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}-ff_{*})d\sigma.

Here
f^{\prime}=f(v^{\prime}),f^{\prime}_{*}=f(v^{\prime}_{*}),f_{*}=f(v_{*})
(with
t
and
x
being parameters only), and

(1.3)

\begin{cases}v^{\prime}&=\dfrac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\dfrac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma\\[10.0pt]
v^{\prime}_{*}&=\dfrac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\dfrac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma\end{cases},

where
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and
(v,v_{*}),\ (v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote the pre-collision and post-collision velocities of two monatomic molecules; see Grad’s work
[
23
]
and the references therein. Moreover, the above formulae express the conservation laws of momentum and kinetic energy for elastic collisions

(1.4)

\begin{cases}&v^{\prime}+v^{\prime}_{*}=v+v_{*}\\[3.0pt]
&|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}=|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}\end{cases}.

We shall use the notation

(1.5)

k=\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|},\quad k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta,\ \ 0\leq\theta\leq\pi.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the support of
B(v-v_{*},\sigma)
lies in the set
{0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2}
, i.e.,
(v-v_{*})\cdot\sigma\geq 0
. Otherwise, this case can be recovered by replacing
B
with its symmetrized version

\bar{B}(v-v_{*},\sigma)=\left[B(v-v_{*},\sigma)+B(v-v_{*},-\sigma)\right]\mathbf{1}_{(v-v_{*})\cdot\sigma>0}.

In this paper, we are primarily concerned with the case where

B
exhibits an angular singularity such that for a generic relative velocity
z
, the integral satisfies

(1.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(z,\sigma)d\sigma=+\infty.

A typical example is the inverse
s
-power law in dimension
3
, where the collision kernel is given by

(1.7)

B(z,\sigma)=|z|^{\gamma}b(k\cdot\sigma),\quad\text{with }\gamma=1-\frac{4}{s-1}

and the angular function satisfies the asymptotic behavior

(1.8)

\sin\theta\,b(\cos\theta)\sim K\theta^{-1-\nu}\text{ as }\theta\rightarrow 0,\quad\nu=\frac{2}{s-1},\quad K>0.

Here the range
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
corresponds to hard potentials, while the range
-3<\gamma<0
refers to soft potentials, and
\gamma=-3
represents the Coulomb potential. Specifically, within the hard potential regime,
\gamma=0
denotes Maxwell molecules and
\gamma=1
represents hard spheres, i.e., the case
s=\infty
with
\sin\theta\,b(\cos\theta)\sim K\theta^{-1}\text{ as }\theta\rightarrow 0
. This paper focuses on the case where
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
which necessitates the condition
5\leq s\leq\infty
. Moreover, one has
\nu\in[0,1/2]
for
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
.

For a given collision kernel
B
, Alexandre-Villani
[
6
]
introduced the notion of momentum transfer
M
as

(1.9)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(v-v^{\prime}\right)d\sigma=\frac{1}{2}(v-v_{*})M(|v-v_{*}|).

Noting that
v-v^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(v-v_{*})-\frac{1}{2}|v_{*}-v|\sigma
, we take the dot product of both sides of (
1.9
) with
v-v_{*}
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|v-v_{*}|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma=\frac{1}{2}|v-v_{*}|^{2}M(|v-v_{*}|).

Consequently, the momentum transfer
M
admits the equivalent representation

(1.10)

M(|z|)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma.

Similarly, we define

(1.11)

M^{\prime}(|z|)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\prime}(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma,

where

(1.12)

B^{\prime}(z,\sigma)=\sup_{1<\lambda\leq\sqrt{2}}\frac{|B(\lambda z,\sigma)-B(z,\sigma)|}{(\lambda-1)|z|}.

The magnitude of the non-negative function
M^{\prime}
provides a very mild control on the regularity of
B
with respect to the relative velocity variable. Furthermore, it was shown in
[
6
]
that appropriate assumptions on
M
and
M^{\prime}
give a precise meaning to the renormalized formulation of the inhomogeneous Boltzmann equation.

1.2.
Properties of momentum transfer

To study the general case of long-range interactions, Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
introduced several assumptions on the collision kernel. For the inverse
s
-power law potentials with hard and soft interactions, the collision kernel naturally satisfies these assumptions, which correspond to Properties I and II detailed below.

Property I: Local Integrability.

We verify that the functions
M(|z|)
and
|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)
corresponding to the model case (
1.7
) are locally integrable. Applying the spherical coordinate transformation yields

(1.13)

\displaystyle M(|z|)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}B(|z|,\cos\theta)(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta d\theta.

Recalling the expression (
1.7
) for the collision kernel
B
and (
1.8
), we conclude that

(1.14)

\displaystyle M(|z|)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi|z|^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}b(\cos\theta)(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta d\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CK\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\theta^{\nu-1}}d\theta|z|^{\gamma}=C_{\nu,K}|z|^{\gamma}.

Similarly, we have

(1.15)

\displaystyle|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)

\displaystyle=|z|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\left[\sup_{1<\lambda\leq\sqrt{2}}\frac{B(\lambda z,\sigma)-B(z,\sigma)}{(\lambda-1)|z|}\right](1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma

\displaystyle=2\pi|z|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\left[\sup_{1<\lambda\leq\sqrt{2}}\frac{|\lambda z|^{\gamma}-|z|^{\gamma}}{(\lambda-1)|z|}\right]b(\cos\theta)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\sin\theta d\theta

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}K\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\theta^{\nu-1}}d\theta|z|^{\gamma}=C^{\prime}_{\nu,K}|z|^{\gamma}.

Due to the local integrability of
|z|^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in(-3,1]
, we conclude that
M(|z|),|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\in L^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z})
.

Property II:
M(|z|)
and
|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)
are both
O(|z|^{\gamma})
as
|z|\rightarrow\infty
.

Following the same reasoning as in (
1.14
) and (
1.15
), we derive that

(1.16)

\displaystyle M(|z|)

\displaystyle\sim K|z|^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\theta^{\nu-1}}d\theta,

\displaystyle|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)

\displaystyle\sim K|z|^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\theta^{\nu-1}}d\theta,

where
\gamma=1-4/(s-1)
and
\nu=\frac{2}{s-1}
. Consequently, both
M(|z|)
and
|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)
are of order
O(|z|^{\gamma})
as
|z|\rightarrow\infty
.

1.3.
Notations and main results

1.3.1.
Notations

In this paper, the symbol
\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the three-dimensional Euclidean space. To distinguish between variables in specific contexts, we attach subscript indices to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(e.g.,
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
,
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
, etc.). Let
B_{R}
denotes the closed balls of radius
R
centered at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To indicate the variable under consideration, we employ the notation
B_{R}(x)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
,
B_{R}(v)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
, etc. The symbol
\mathbb{R}_{+}
denotes the positive half-line defined by
\mathbb{R}_{+}=\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{1};t>0\}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{S}^{2}=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{3};|\sigma|=1\}
represents the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The set of non-negative integers is denoted by
\mathbb{N}
, and we define
\mathbb{N}^{k}=\{\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{k})|n_{i}\in\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq k\}
for any integer
k\geq 1
. For any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, its
1
-norm is defined by
|\mathbf{n}|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
. The abbreviation “a.e.” stands for “almost everywhere”. The symbol
L^{p}(d\mu)
represents the standard
L^{p}
space associated with the measure
\mu
. For any functional space
F
, we write
F_{+}=\{f\in F;f\geq 0\textrm{ a.e.}\}
. The notation
f^{n}\rightrightarrows f
indicates the uniform convergence of
f^{n}
to
f
, while
f^{n}\rightharpoonup f
denotes the weak convergence of
f^{n}
to
f
in the weak
L^{1}
topology. The Fourier transform operator is denoted by
\mathcal{F}
; the Fourier transform of
f
is occasionally written as
\hat{f}
, so that
\mathcal{F}f=\hat{f}
. We write
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
. Furthermore, we introduce the weighted norm
\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(v)(1+|v|^{\alpha})dv
. We also introduce the space
L\log L
as

\displaystyle L\log L=\{f:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\to\mathbb{R}_{+};\ f|\log f|\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\}\,.

For any smooth function
f(t,x,v)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
and any multi-index
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{6})\in\mathbb{N}^{7}
, we define the partial derivative operator
D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
by

\displaystyle D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}f=\frac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|}f}{\partial t^{\zeta_{0}}\partial x_{1}^{\zeta_{1}}\partial x_{2}^{\zeta_{2}}\partial x_{3}^{\zeta_{3}}\partial v_{1}^{\zeta_{4}}\partial v_{2}^{\zeta_{5}}\partial v_{3}^{\zeta_{6}}}\,.

Let
u:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous bounded function. For any exponent
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the supremum norm and the
\alpha
-Hölder semi-norm are defined, respectively, by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}|u(x)|,\quad[u]_{C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

The Hölder space
C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
consists of all functions with finite
\alpha
-Hölder norm

C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}):=\left\{u:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\to\mathbb{R}\ \middle|\ \|u\|_{C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}:=\|u\|_{C(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}+[u]_{C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}<\infty\right\}.

We denote by
C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
the closure of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
under the
C^{\alpha}
norm. Following Remark 2.2.2 in
[
25
]
and Proposition 2.76 in
[
29
]
, the dual space of
C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
is identified with the Besov space
B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
.
To define this space properly, we introduce the Littlewood-Paley decomposition. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the annulus
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi}\mid 3/4\leq|\xi|\leq 8/3\}
. There exist radial smooth functions
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(B(0,4/3))
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{C})
taking values in
[0,1]
, such that

\displaystyle\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi},\quad\chi(\xi)+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varphi(2^{-j}\xi)=1,

\displaystyle\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi}\setminus\{0\},\quad\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(2^{-j}\xi)=1,

\displaystyle|j-j^{\prime}|\geq 2\implies\operatorname{supp}

\displaystyle\varphi(2^{-j}\cdot)\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi(2^{-j^{\prime}}\cdot)=\emptyset,

\displaystyle j\geq 1\implies\operatorname{supp}

\displaystyle\chi\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi(2^{-j}\cdot)=\emptyset.

Let
h=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi
and
\tilde{h}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\chi
. The nonhomogeneous dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
are defined as follows

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}u=0\quad\text{if}\quad j\leq-2,

\displaystyle\Delta_{-1}u=\chi(D)u=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{y}}\tilde{h}(y)u(x-y)\,dy,

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}u=\varphi(2^{-j}D)u=2^{3j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{y}}h(2^{j}y)u(x-y)\,dy\quad\text{if}\quad j\geq 0.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the nonhomogeneous Besov space
B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
consists of all tempered distributions
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
such that

\|u\|_{B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}:=\sum_{j\geq-1}2^{-j\alpha}\|\Delta_{j}u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})}<\infty.

Furthermore, a function
f
belongs to the space
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})))
provided that for every test function
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
, the mapping

t\mapsto\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(t,x,v)\,\varphi(x,v)\,dx\,dv

is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

1.3.2.
Initial data

In this paper, we focus on the Cauchy problem of the equation (
1.1
), whose initial data is

(1.17)

f(t,x,v)|_{t=0}=f_{0}(x,v)\geq 0,\quad\text{a.e. on }\ \mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}

with the following assumptions:

(1.18)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f_{0}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f_{0}|)dxdv<\infty\,.

1.3.3.
Definition of renormalized solutions

Definition 1.1

(Renormalized solution)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\nu,1)
with
\nu
defined in (
1.8
). A function

(1.19)

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)\in C\big(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))\big)\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv)

is a renormalized solution of (
1.1
) if

•

f(t,x,v)\geq 0

a.e.
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
;

•

for every
\beta\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that

(1.20)

\displaystyle\beta(0)=0,\quad 0<\beta^{\prime}(t)\leq C/(1+t),\quad\beta^{\prime\prime}(t)\leq 0

for some
C>0
, the function
\beta(f)
solves

(1.21)

\frac{\partial\beta(f)}{\partial t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(f)=\beta^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)

in the sense of distributions.

Remark 1.1
.

Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
introduced a different definition of renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) with defect measure, in which equation (
1.21
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\beta(f)}{\partial t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(f)\geq\beta^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)\,,

which holds in the sense of distributions.

Next, we define the Boltzmann-type H-functional by

(1.22)

H(f)(t)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f\log fdxdv.

We are now in a position to state the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
and
\nu<\alpha<1
with
\nu\in[0,1/2]
defined in (
1.8
). Suppose that the initial data
f_{0}
satisfies (
1.17
) and (
1.18
). Then the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.17
) admits a renormalized solution

f\in C\big(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))\big)\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv)

in the sense of Definition
1.1
, satisfying the following statements:

(1)

For any
T>0
and any
t\in[0,T]
, there exists a constant
C_{T}>0
such that

(1.23)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(t,\cdot)(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)dxdv\leq C_{T}\,.

(2)

The following entropy inequality holds:

(1.24)

H(f)(t)+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}ds\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B({f}^{\prime}{f^{\prime}_{*}}-ff_{*})\log\left(\frac{{f}^{\prime}{f^{\prime}_{*}}}{ff_{*}}\right)dxdvdv_{*}d\sigma\leq H(f_{0})\,.

Remark 1.2

(Soft potential model
-3<\gamma<0
)
.

The smoothing estimate in Lemma
5.4
and the average time-velocity estimate in Lemma
7.1
are necessitated in proving the existence of renormalized solutions. To establish these two estimates, one needs to control the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}(v)f^{n}(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
. However, in Example
9.1
, we construct a counterexample showing that there exist a positive function
f(v)
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)dv<\infty
such that
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f(v)f(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}=+\infty
. Consequently, the approach developed for the hard potential case fails to solve the soft potential model in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

The collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}(v)f^{n}(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
can be bounded in terms of
\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}
with
p=\frac{6}{6+\gamma}
for
-3<\gamma<0
. Noting that
H^{\nu/2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, we thus obtain a bound on this collision integral provided that the
H^{\nu/2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
-norm of
f^{n}
is uniformly bounded. For the hard potential case
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
, such a bound was established in
[
5
]
. However, the corresponding estimate for soft potential fails, as the
H^{\nu/2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
-norm of
f^{n}
would need to be controlled by the collision integral itself, resulting in a circular dependence.

1.4.
Comparison with the work
[
6
]
of Alexandre and Villani

In this subsection, we discuss the differences between the present work and that of Alexandre and Villani’s work
[
6
]
.

First, the model (
1.1
) considered in this paper differs from that studied by Alexandre and Villani in
[
6
]
. Indeed, Alexandre and Villani imposed decay assumptions on the momentum transfer
M(|z|)
and the mild regular momentum transfer
M^{\prime}(|v|)
at infinity,

(1.25)

\displaystyle M(|z|)=o(1)\,,\quad|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)=o(|z|^{2})\quad\textrm{as }\ |z|\to\infty\,,

see (2.9) on Page 42 of
[
6
]
1
1

1

In fact, they introduced a weighted momentum transfer
M^{\alpha}(|z|)
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 2
defined by

\displaystyle M^{\alpha}(|z|)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}d\sigma\,,\quad k=\frac{z}{|z|}\,,

which satisfies
M^{2}(|z|)=M(|z|)
, see (
1.13
). They then assumed that for
\alpha\in[0,2]
, as
|z|\to\infty
,

\displaystyle M^{\alpha}(|z|)=o(|z|^{2-\alpha})\,.

. As shown in (
1.16
), the momentum transfer
M(|z|)
and the mild regular momentum transfer
M^{\prime}(|v|)
in the present work satisfy

\displaystyle M(|z|)=O(|z|^{\gamma})\,,\quad|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)=O(|z|^{\gamma})\quad\textrm{as }\ |z|\to+\infty

for
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
. Namely, the model considered here does not satisfy the assumptions (
1.25
) of Alexandre and Villani, which require faster decay at infinity.

In
[
6
]
, assumptions (
1.25
) at infinity are utilized to establish the integrability of the
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
-part of the renormalized collision operator
\beta^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)
; see (
4.3
) below. In this procedure, for given
v_{*}
, the linear operator (
4.7
), namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:\varphi\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)(\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi)d\sigma

plays a key role in proving the integrability of
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
. Here
\mathcal{T}
is the adjoint operator of
f\mapsto Q(\delta_{v_{*}},f)
. Alexandre and Villani proved that
\mathcal{T}
is bounded from
W^{2,\infty}
to
L^{\infty}
. Namely, for all
\varphi\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3})
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}\varphi(v)|\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\|\varphi\|_{W^{2,\infty}}|v-v_{*}|(1+\tfrac{|v-v_{*}|}{2})M(|v-v_{*}|)\,,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}\varphi(v)|\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\varphi\|_{W^{2,\infty}}(1+|v-v_{*}|)^{\alpha}M^{\alpha}(|v-v_{*}|)\quad\forall\ \alpha\in[0,2]\,,

see Proposition 3.3 on Page 52 of
[
6
]
. Owing to the polynomial factors
|v-v_{*}|(1+\tfrac{|v-v_{*}|}{2})
and
(1+|v-v_{*}|)^{\alpha}
in the above bounds, the assumptions (
1.25
) at infinity are requisite for establishing the integrability of
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
by means of the a priori bound (
1.23
).

In our work, for the hard potential model (
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
),
M(|z|)
and
|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)
do not exhibit decay properties at infinity (see (
1.16
)). To overcome this difficulty, we establish the boundedness of the operator
\mathcal{T}
form
C_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3})
(
\alpha\in(\nu,1)
) to
L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3})
, i.e., for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3})
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}\varphi(v)|\leq C_{\nu,\alpha}\|\varphi\|_{C_{c}^{\alpha}}|v-v_{*}|^{\alpha+\gamma}\leq C^{\prime}_{\nu,\alpha}\|\varphi\|_{C_{c}^{\alpha}}|v-v_{*}|^{\alpha}M(|v-v_{*}|)\,,

see Lemma
4.3
below. The integrability of
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
is then established by means of the a priori bound (
1.23
).

Second, we establish a so-called
average time-velocity estimates for
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dv\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu,R}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})dv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv\text{ a.e.}\ x\in\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}

for
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
(see (
7.1
) in Lemma
7.1
below), where
e^{n}
denotes the entropy dissipative density defined in (
5.1
) below. We also establish a so-called
uniform smallness estimate for small angular deflections

\displaystyle\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma dv\leq\frac{C}{k}(1+|v_{*}|)^{1+\gamma+\nu}\|g\|^{2}_{{H^{\nu/2}}{(B_{R}(v))}}

for
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
(see (
7.13
) in Lemma
7.4
below). These two estimates constitute the most essential observation for removing the defect measure appearing in the work of Alexandre-Villani
[
6
]
. The defect measure arises from Fatou lemma argument

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})dxdv\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dxdv

under the strong convergence
f^{n}\to f
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3})
, corresponding to the arguments on Page 61 of
[
6
]
.

In our approach, we observe that

\displaystyle f^{n}_{*}\frac{({f^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n})^{2}}{(1+f^{n})^{2}(1+{f^{n}}^{\prime})}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}_{*}}\right)^{2}\,,

which implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dv\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}_{*}}\right)^{2}dvdv_{*}d\sigma\,.

Moreover, combining the cancellation lemma (see Lemma
4.1
below) with the lower bound (
7.4
) on the entropy dissipative density, i.e.,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}_{*}}\right)^{2}dvdv_{*}d\sigma

\displaystyle+\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}({f^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n})({f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n}_{*})dvdv_{*}d\sigma\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv,

we successfully establish the average time-velocity estimate. By using the decomposition
v^{\prime}-v=h=h_{1}+h_{2}
, where
h_{1}=-u\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta}{2})
,
h_{2}=|u|\sin(\frac{\theta}{2})\cos(\frac{\theta}{2})\omega
,
u=v-v_{*}
, and
\omega\perp u
, we obtain the change of variables formula

d\sigma=\frac{4}{|u|^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{4}{|u|^{2}}|h_{2}|^{2}}}dh_{2}\,.

Together with the asymptotic behavior
\theta\thicksim\frac{2}{|u|}|h_{2}|
and
b(\cos\theta)\thicksim K\theta^{-2-\nu}
as
\theta\to 0^{+}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)(g(v^{\prime})-g(v))^{2}d\sigma dv

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}(1+|v_{*}|^{1+\gamma+\nu})\int_{B_{R}(v)}dv\int_{|h|\leq\sqrt{2}r_{k}}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h|^{3+\nu}}dh\,.

Combined with the equivalent relation

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\approx\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{(f(v^{\prime})-f(v))^{2}}{|v^{\prime}-v|^{3+2s}}\mathbf{1}_{|v-v^{\prime}|\leq 1}dvdv^{\prime}

given in Section 7.3 of
[
26
]
, we establish the uniform smallness estimate for small angular deflections. Upon obtaining these estimates, we derive the equi-integrability of the sequence
\{\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{R_{v}^{3}}(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}f^{n}dv)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv\}_{n\geq 1}
via the renormalized form (
7.7
) of the approximated equation, where the test function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3};C_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}))
has compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
; see Lemma
7.2
below.

In order to remove the defect measure, we must derive the convergence (
7.14
) in Lemma
7.5
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})\varphi dxdv\,.

The idea is to decompose
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
into the angular singularity part
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}
,
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}
and far-field angular singularity part
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
,
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}
, respectively. We introduce the quantities

\displaystyle M^{n}(x)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(1+|v|^{2})dv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv\,,

and

\displaystyle M(x)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|v|^{2})dv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv\,,

and define the sets

\displaystyle N_{L}^{n}=\{x\in B_{R}(x):M^{n}(x)>L\}\,,\quad N_{L}=\{x\in B_{R}(x):M(x)>L\}\,.

By the uniform bound (
3.12
) in Lemma
3.5
, the measures of these sets satisfy
\textrm{meas}(N_{L}^{n})+\textrm{meas}(N_{L})\leq\frac{C}{L}
uniformly in
n\geq 1
. On the other hand, the average time-velocity estimate (
7.1
) shows that the sequence
\{\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{R_{v}^{3}}(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}f^{n}dv)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dv\}_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly bounded in
n\geq 1
and
x\in(N_{L}^{n})^{c}=\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}\setminus N_{L}^{n}
. By uniform boundednes and equi-integrability of this sequence, we obtain the convergence (
7.12
) for the far-field angular singularity part
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
since

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}^{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)f_{*}^{n}\Gamma(f^{n},{f^{n}}^{\prime})\,dv_{*}d\sigma\leq C(f^{n}+{f^{n}}^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}\,dv_{*}

and the Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem; see the proof of procedure of Lemma
7.3
below. Then, using the average time-velocity estimate and uniform smallness estimate for small angular deflections, we prove that the angular singularity parts
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{n,k}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}
vanish in the limit
k\to\infty
, namely,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\right)\varphi dxdv=0;

see (
7.17
) below. Then we establish the convergence

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})\varphi\,dxdv;

see the proof of procedure of Lemma
7.5
below. Therefore, the convergence (
7.14
) is established and the defect measure is removed.

Although the hard potential model (
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
) considered in this paper differs from that of Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
, our approach can also eliminate the defect measure appearing in
[
6
]
when
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
.

Third, the existence of renormalized solutions to the soft potential model
(-3<\gamma<0)
fails because the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v_{*}}^{3}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
is uncontrollable. When deriving the smoothing estimate for
\mathcal{F}(\sqrt{f^{n}\chi_{R}})
in Lemma
5.4
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi|\geq 1}|\mathcal{F}(\sqrt{f^{n}}\chi_{R})|^{2}Z_{n}\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{0}C_{2}J^{2}(2\pi)^{3}}{\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v)}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}(t,x,v)dv+\frac{C_{1}}{r_{0}^{2}}\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}+\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}f^{n}({f^{n}_{*}}^{\prime}-f^{n}_{*})dvdv_{*}d\sigma\right\},

the quantity
\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}f^{n}({f^{n}_{*}}^{\prime}-f^{n}_{*})dvdv_{*}d\sigma
in the right-hand side of the above inequality is bounded by the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v_{*}}^{3}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
via the cancellation lemma (Lemma
4.1
). In deriving the average time-velocity estimate, the cancellation lemma is also employed. Then the quantity
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dv
is bounded by

\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\,dv\,dv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\!dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}\,dv

for both hard and soft potentials by means of the cancellation lemma; see (
7.6
) and Remark
7.1
below. Consequently, we must control the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v_{*}}^{3}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
for the soft potential model
-3<\gamma<0
. Unfortunately, we construct a counterexample showing that there exist a non-negative function
f(v)
with finite mass, energy, and entropy

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}f(1+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)dv<\infty\,,

such the collision integral

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v_{*}}^{3}}f(v)f(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}=+\infty\,,

see Example
9.1
below. Therefore, the approach developed for the hard potential model in this paper does not apply to the soft potential model.

In fact, the soft potential case
-3<\gamma<0
in the work of Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
encounters the same obstacle. When proving the strong convergence of
f^{n}
in the
L^{1}
sense, they employed the following estimate (corresponding to the estimate in Lemma
5.4
of the present work)

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi|\geq 1}|\mathcal{F}\sqrt{f^{n}_{R}}|^{2}Z_{n}(\tfrac{1}{|\xi|})d\xi\leq C(f^{n},R,\Phi_{0})[D(f^{n})+\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{2}}]\,,

see (4.4) on Page 58 of
[
6
]
. This estimate is cited from the work of Alexandre, Desvillettes, Villani and Wennberg
[
5
]
(Theorem 1 on Page 331-332). Note that in
[
5
]
, the assumption
M(|z|)+|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\leq C_{0}(1+|z|)^{2}
is required
2
2

2

They used the notation
\Lambda(|z|)+|z|\Lambda^{\prime}(|z|)\leq C_{0}(1+|z|)^{2}
, where
\Lambda(|z|)
and
\Lambda^{\prime}(|z|)
were defined in (14) and (15) on Page 331 of
[
5
]
, repectively, which coincide with the same definitions of
M(|z|)
and
M^{\prime}(|z|)
in the present work.
. In
[
6
]
, the authors only assumed
M(|z|),|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
(see Page 42 of
[
6
]
), which includes the case
M(|z|)+|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\leq C_{1}|z|^{\gamma}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for
-N<\gamma<0
. In this case, the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{v_{*}}^{3}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}
remains uncontrollable. For the collision kernel
B(z,\sigma)=|z|^{\gamma}b(k\cdot\sigma)
considered in this paper, the condition
M(|z|)+|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\leq C_{0}(1+|z|)^{2}
corresponds to the hard potential
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
, while
M(|z|)+|z|M^{\prime}(|z|)\leq C_{1}|z|^{\gamma}
corresponds to soft potential
-3<\gamma<0
.

1.5.
Historical remarks.

Research on the Boltzmann equation and related models can generally be classified into two main categories: the regime of classical solutions and the regime of renormalized solutions. In the framework of classical solutions, works typically rely on the assumption of small initial data (see, for instance,
[
24
,
27
,
28
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
36
,
48
]
). The following review focuses primarily on the regime of renormalized solutions.

In the early stage of studying the Boltzmann equation, the lack of global a priori estimates for solutions and their derivatives limited the analysis. As a result, most works focused on the spatially homogeneous case, such as
[
1
,
2
,
4
,
44
]
. Later, Kaniel and Shinbrot
[
39
]
extended the study to the spatially inhomogeneous setting in bounded domains. They constructed the approximated solutions controlled by a local Maxwellian distribution and proved the existence and uniqueness of solutions to the initial boundary value problem for a short time interval.

A milestone breakthrough in this field began in 1989, when DiPerna and Lions
[
10
]
introduced the renormalized solution to the transported equations. Subsequently, in
[
11
]
they established the existence theory of global renormalized solutions for the classical Boltzmann equation satisfying Grad’s angular cutoff assumption with large initial data in the whole space. In
[
12
]
, they proved that the renormalized solutions to the cutoff Boltzmann equation satisfy the entropy inequality. However, for long-range interactions, such as those described by the hard and soft potential models, the collision cross section exhibits non-integrable singularities primarily in the angular variable. In this case, the collision kernel
B(v-v_{*},\sigma)
is not integrable with respect to the angular variable. Such singular behavior lies beyond the scope of the DiPerna-Lions theory.

Focusing on cutoff models, Lions studied the convergence behavior of solution sequences in the series works
[
41
,
42
]
. He showed that the sequence of solutions
f^{n}
is strongly compact in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
if and only if the initial data sequence
f_{0}^{n}
is strongly compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. Based on this result, Lions proposed a conjecture that this reversible propagation of convergence comes from the angular cutoff assumption in the collision operator. In contrast, for non-cutoff models such as the Landau equation, the evolution process itself may create a regularizing effect. This means that the solution sequence can become strongly compact even when the initial data do not have this property. Subsequently, in
[
40
]
, Lions studied the Cauchy problem for the Landau equation and confirmed this idea. He proved that the solution sequence of the Landau equation enjoys strong compactness, while showing that such a property does not hold for the Boltzmann equation under the angular cutoff assumption. Inspired by these results, Villani
[
49
]
proved in 1996 the global existence of renormalized solutions for the Landau equation with defect measure.

Regarding the regularity of the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation, Desvillettes
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
carried out a systematic study of its regularizing effects. He first established the theory for the two-dimensional equation with radially symmetric Maxwellian molecules. He then extended these results to the case of radially symmetric, velocity-dependent cross sections, and further to the case of non-radially symmetric Maxwellian molecules.

In terms of entropy dissipation estimates, Villani
[
50
]
employed a decomposition of the entropy dissipation together with the Carleman representation to show, for the first time, that under the assumption of a local lower bound on
f
, the Sobolev norm of
\sqrt{f}
can be controlled by the entropy dissipation. Subsequently, Alexandre, Desvillettes, Villani, and Wennberg
[
5
]
refined these conclusions using tools such as Fourier analysis, successfully providing a complete proof without the lower bound assumption. Building on this, Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
introduced renormalized solutions with defect measures for the Boltzmann equation with long-range interactions. They established the global existence of such renormalized solutions and proved their strong compactness, thus confirming Lions’ conjecture.

The works mentioned above on renormalized solutions were all carried out in the whole space
\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}
. For bounded domains with various boundary conditions, several results have been obtained so far. The first breakthrough was achieved by Mischler
[
46
]
in 2010, who proved the existence of renormalized solutions to the cutoff Boltzmann equation, Vlasov-Poisson and Fokker-Planck type models, in bounded domains with Maxwell reflection boundary conditions. In 2019, Jiang-Zhang investigated renormalized solutions to the
Boltzmann equation with angular cutoff model
[
37
]
, and renormalized solutions with a non-negative defect measure to the Boltzmann equation without angular cutoff model
[
38
]
, both in bounded domains with incoming boundary conditions.

In the past three decades, the hydrodynamic limit of the Boltzmann equation has attracted considerable attention. Research in the framework of renormalized solutions began with the work of Bardos, Golse, and Levermore in the 1990s
[
7
,
8
]
, who established a unified framework linking the DiPerna-Lions renormalized solutions of the Boltzmann equation with the Leray weak solutions of the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. Building on this foundation, Golse and Saint-Raymond
[
21
,
22
]
achieved a major breakthrough by rigorously deriving macroscopic fluid equations from the microscopic kinetic description without relying on any nonlinear weak compactness assumptions. For problems on bounded spatial domains, serval related works have also been developed within the renormalized solutions framework. In
[
45
]
, Masmoudi and Saint-Raymond proved the linear Stokes limit from fluctuations of renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain. In
[
47
]
, Saint-Raymond rigorously justified the Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit in the renormalized solutions regime over a bounded domain. In
[
45
,
47
]
, the fluid boundary conditions were either the Dirichlet or the Navier slip boundary condition, depending on the relative sizes of the accommodation coefficient and the Knudsen number
Kn
. In
[
35
]
, Jiang and Masmoudi rigorously proved the Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit with Neumann boundary condition from renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain with Maxwell reflection boundary condition for small accommodation coefficient
\sqrt{Kn}
. Moreover, Jiang and Zhang
[
37
,
38
]
rigorously verified the Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit from renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation (including both cutoff and non-cutoff cases) in a bounded domain with incoming boundary condition.

1.6.
Sketch of proofs

In this subsection, we mainly sketch the ideas of proving the main
theorem. In 2002, Alexandre and Villani
[
6
]
established the existence of renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation without angular cutoff in the whole space, introducing a non-negative defect measure.
Due to the singularity of the collision kernel in the angular variable, the classical approach of splitting the collision operator into “gain” and “loss” terms is no longer valid. To overcome this, the renormalized collision operator
\beta^{\prime}_{\delta}(f)Q(f,f)
is decomposed into three components:
(\mathcal{R}_{1})
,
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
, and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
. Consequently, the existence of renormalized solutions reduces to establishing the convergence of the approximated operators
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}
,
(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}
, and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. Among these, the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
presents the primary difficulty. In earlier works, due to the absence of sufficient a priori estimates for this term, one could only apply Fatou’s lemma (Lemma
2.5
) to obtain lower semicontinuity, which naturally leads to the appearance of a non-negative defect measure in the solution.
However, for hard potential models, we observe that by expanding the squared term and utilizing the entropy inequality, one can derive the key average time-velocity estimate for
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. The equi-integrability of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}dv
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}dv
, together with the strong convergence of
f^{n}
, then implies the equi-integrability of
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi\,dv
on
B_{R}(x)
. To address the singularity at
\theta=0
, we decompose the spherical integral of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
into singular and non-singular regions. We establish the convergence of the non-singular part and show that the singular part converges uniformly to zero. Combining these results, we obtain the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. This advancement enables us to prove the global existence of renormalized solutions for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation with hard potentials in the standard sense, without requiring any defect measure.

Step 1. Construction of smooth approximation problem (
3.1
).

We begin by constructing a smooth approximation problem (
3.1
) for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation.
The approximate initial data
f_{0}^{n}
are taken in the Schwartz space. They satisfy a Gaussian lower bound and converge to the original initial data
f_{0}
in entropy and in weighted norms, see Lemma
3.1
. This choice provides sufficient regularity and ensures consistency with the original problem.
To handle the angular singularity associated with long range interactions, we introduce a cutoff collision kernel
B_{n}(z,\sigma)
. The angular part
b_{n}(\cos\theta)
is modified by a smooth function depending on
n\sin(\theta/2)
. This modification removes the contribution of grazing collisions when
\theta
is close to zero.

Based on this modified kernel
B_{n}(z,\sigma)
, one can construct the approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}
in (
3.5
). Note that the factor
\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}fdv\right)^{-1}
is such that the nonlinear approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
admits linear upper bounds in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
as in Lemma
3.3
and ensures Lipschitz continuity in the
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
space. These properties imply the existence, uniqueness, and positivity of a global smooth solution
f^{n}
to the approximation problem (
3.1
).
Finally, the approximate solutions preserve the fundamental physical properties of the Boltzmann equation, including the conservation of mass, momentum, and energy. Besides, an entropy identity is also satisfied and provides uniform in
n
bounds on entropy and velocity moments, see Lemma
3.5
. These uniform estimates supply the compactness required for the limit procedure.

Step 2. Well-definition of the renormalized collision operator.

To establish the validity of the collision operator for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation, we adopt the renormalization method. We first decompose the renormalized collision term
\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)
into three distinct parts, denoted by
(\mathcal{R}_{1}),\ (\mathcal{R}_{2})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
. This decomposition allows us to estimate each term separately.
First, we analyze the term
(\mathcal{R}_{1})
. This term involves a singular integral operator defined as
\mathscr{S}f
. By applying the Cancellation lemma (Lemma
4.1
), we effectively handles the singularity and proves
(\mathcal{R}_{1})\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];L^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times B_{R}(v)\right)\right),\ \forall R>0
.
Subsequently, to address the critical case, we employ test functions in the Hölder space
C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
with
\nu<\alpha<1
. Utilizing the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity, we control the singularity of the collision kernel and estimate the bilinear term
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
directly. This leads to a more regular dual space compared to the
W^{1,\infty}_{0}
framework. Specifically, we show that
(\mathcal{R}_{2})\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];L^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(B_{R}(v))\right)\right).

Finally, we handle the integrability of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
. This term arises from the convexity of the renormalization function
\beta_{\delta}(f)
and is strictly non-negative.
We multiply the renormalized Boltzmann equation by a test function
\varphi
and integrate it over
t,x,v
. Together with the bounds for
(\mathcal{R}_{1})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
obtained earlier, we deduce that
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
must also be finite in
L^{1}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times B_{R}(v)\right)
. Thus, the renormalized collision operator is well-defined as a distribution.

Step 3. Strong compactness of approximated solution.
By the uniform bound (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) of
f^{n}
and the Dunford-Pettis theorem (Lemma
2.1
), it follows directly that
f^{n}
converges weakly to
f
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
.
To establish that the approximated solution
f^{n}
converges strongly to
f
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, we proceed in several steps. First, based on the preliminary lemmas, we establish the functional inequality (
5.3
), which shows that the
high-frequency components of
\sqrt{f^{n}}
in the velocity variable are controlled by the entropy dissipation.

Next, applying the averaged velocity lemma (Lemma
2.10
), we derive that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv
is compact in the space
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
.
Subsequently, combining the reflexivity of
L^{p}(1<p<\infty)
spaces, we consider the weak limit
g
of
g^{n}=\sqrt{f^{n}}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))
. The discussion is divided into two cases. For almost every
t,x,v
satisfying
g(t,x,v)=0
, the weak convergence property and the non-negativity of
f^{n}
imply that
\sqrt{f^{n}}
converges strongly to zero. For almost all points satisfying
g(t,x,v)>0
, the smoothing estimate (
5.3
) implies that the high-frequency components of the solution vanish uniformly. Accordingly, combined with the properties of the Fourier transform and the M. Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov Theorem (Lemma
2.8
), we prove that
\sqrt{f^{n}}
converges strongly in
L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))
. This implies that
f^{n}
converges in measure. Combining the weak compactness of
f^{n}
, the uniform bound (
3.11
) and the Vitali Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.2
), we prove that
f^{n}
converges strongly to
f
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. Furthermore, we give a counterexample showing that the above method is not applicable to soft potentials with
-3<\gamma<0
. Even if finite entropy and the standard conservation laws hold, the distribution function may still develop local concentration. When this behavior interacts with the singular factor
|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}
, the collision frequency can diverge. Consequently, the corresponding upper bound becomes infinite, i.e., the argument fails in the soft potential case.

Step 4. The convergence of renormalized collision operator.
Since renormalized collision operator
\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
can be decomposed into three sidtinct parts, denoted by
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n},(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
, verifying the convergence of
\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
reduces to verifying the convergence of the decomposed terms
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n},\ (\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. To study the convergence of the renormalized collision operator
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}
, we truncate the velocity variable
v_{*}
. This allows us to split the estimates into two parts: one on compact sets and one at infinity.
Next, by applying the M. Riesz–Fréchet–Kolmogorov theorem (Lemma
2.8
), we prove that the convolution term
f^{n}*_{v}S^{n}
is relatively compact in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times B_{R}(v))
, thereby deriving its strong convergence.
Finally, using the Product Limit Lemma (Lemma
2.7
), we successfully establish the weak convergence (
6.1
) of the term
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{1})\varphi dxdv.

For the limit of the term
(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}
, we split the integral according to the size of the relative velocity
|v-v_{*}|
.
On the truncated region, we use the strong convergence of
f^{n}
and
\beta\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
to obtain weak compactness of
\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}f^{n}_{*}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})
. Together with the distributional convergence of the operator
\mathcal{T}^{n}
, this allows us to identify the limit.
For large relative velocities, the contribution is controlled by uniform bound (
3.11
) and therefore goes to zero as the truncation radius goes to infinity. Combining the two cases above, the convergence (
6.5
) holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{2})\varphi dxdv.

Finally, we address the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. Specifically, we expand the squared term and combine it with the entropy inequality to derive the average time-velocity estimate (
7.1
) for
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
.
Subsequently, by integrating the renormalized approximate Boltzmann equation (
7.7
) with respect to
t
,
x
, and
v
, and using the established weak compactness of
f^{n}
along with that of the terms
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}\varphi dv
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}\varphi dv
, we obtain the equi-integrability of
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv

on
B_{R}(x)
.

Based on these facts, to address the singularity of the collision kernel at
\theta=0
, we decompose
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
into two parts, denoted by

(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}
. The term
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
corresponds to the truncated collision kernel
B_{n}^{k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{k}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}}
, while
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}
accounts for the singular part of the kernel, defined by
B_{n,k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B_{n}(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\frac{1}{k}}
. Furthermore, we define the functionals
M^{n}(x)
and
M(x)
and the sets
N^{n}_{L}
and
N_{L}
, in (
7.11
). Consequently, the average time-velocity estimate (
7.1
) implies that the integral
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dv
is uniformly bounded on the set
(N_{L}^{n})^{c}
. Combining this bound with the convergence
f^{n}\to f
a.e. in
(t,x,v)
and
B^{k}_{n}\to B^{k}
a.e. in
(v_{*},\sigma)
, we deduce the convergence of the non-singular part
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
, namely

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv.

For the singular part, we aim to prove that the corresponding integral converges uniformly to zero. To this end, we established an estimate (
7.13
) in Lemma
7.4
. This estimate indicates that the angular singularity of the collision kernel at
\theta\approx 0
exhibits fractional derivative behavior. Furthermore, the corresponding integral contribution is controlled by the fractional Sobolev norm
\|g\|_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}
of the distribution function
g
and tends uniformly to zero as the truncation angle decreases (i.e.,
k\to\infty
). Recall that the estimate (
5.3
) implies that for hard potentials, the
H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))
-norm of
\sqrt{f^{n}}
is bounded by

\frac{C_{0}J^{2}(2\pi)^{3}}{\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\left(r_{0}/2\right)^{\gamma}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}(t,x,v)\,dv+\left(C_{\nu}+\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}_{0}}\right)\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\right\}.

Furthermore, utilizing the sets
W_{\epsilon}
and
U_{\delta}
defined in Lemma
5.5
, we decompose the domain
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)
into three parts:
W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})
,
U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n}
and
([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}
. By virtue of the boundedness of
\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{R}(x);H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v)))}
on the set
W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})
, combined with the uniform lower bound of
\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\,dv
on this set for sufficiently large
n
, we deduce that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dxdv=0.

The small measure of the set
U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n}
implies that

\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dxdv\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0,L\to\infty).

Finally, the estimate
\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\leq 2\epsilon\
almost everywhere on
([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}
, together with the average time-velocity estimate for
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
, implies that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}}\,dxdv=0.

Moreover, for any
1\leq n\leq N
, it is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\right)\varphi dxdv=0,\quad\forall 1\leq n\leq N.

These convergence results imply that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\right)\varphi dxdv=0.

Consequently, combining this with the convergence of the non-singular part
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
, we obtain the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
, i.e.,

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})\varphi dxdv.

Step 5. Existence and entropy inequality.

By combining the convergence properties (
6.1
), (
6.5
), and (
7.14
), for any test function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
with compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
, taking the limit
n\to\infty
in the approximating equation yields:

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\beta_{\delta}(f)(T,\cdot)-\beta_{\delta}(f)(0,\cdot))\varphi\,dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)\varphi\,dxdv.

This verifies that
f
is indeed a renormalized solution of the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
). Furthermore, by invoking the absolute continuity of the integral and the convergence relationship between
\beta_{\delta}(f)
and
f
as
\delta\to 0
, it can be deduced that the limit function is continuous in time with respect to the weak topology, i.e.,
f\in C\left(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))\right)_{+}
. In addition, by applying the Monotone Convergence Theorem and the estimates for the
H
-functional
H(f)
, it is shown that
f
satisfies the following uniform bound:

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)\,dxdv\leq C_{T}.

Finally, we establish the entropy inequality (
1.24
). To derive the desired entropy inequality from the approximated entropy identity (
3.10
), it suffices to verify the bound (
8.5
), i.e.,
\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv
. We first utilize the convergence (
5.10
) and the bound
f^{n}\log f^{n}\in L^{\infty}((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
to establish that the sequences
{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}
and
f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}
are relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta )
for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}(x)
. We then can verify that for all
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(E_{R})
,

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\varphi d\Theta\to

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}ff_{*}B^{R}\varphi d\Theta,

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\varphi d\Theta\to

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}{f}^{\prime}f_{*}^{\prime}B^{R}\varphi d\Theta

strongly in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))
, where
N(f)=1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}fdv
. Together with the fact that for any Borel subset
A
of
E_{R}
, there exists a sequence of measurable subsets
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that
\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}\to\mathbf{1}_{A}
in
L^{1}(E_{R})
. We can obtain that for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}(x)
,

\frac{f^{n}f^{n}_{*}}{1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow{f}{f_{*}}B^{R},\quad\frac{{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}{1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow\ f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}B^{R}\quad\text{weakly in }L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta).

At the end, since the function

j(a,b)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}(a-b)\log\frac{a}{b},&\quad\text{for }a,b>0\\
+\infty,&\quad\text{for }a\text{ or }b\leq 0\end{array}\right.

is convex, the property of weak lower semicontinuity implies that
\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv
.
So the entropy inequality (
1.24
) in Theorem
1.1
is obtained.

1.7.
Organizations of current paper

In the next section, we begin by providing several fundamental results from functional analysis that serve as the technical foundation for our subsequent proofs. In Section
3
, we construct the approximate equations, establish the existence and uniqueness of their smooth solutions, and demonstrate that the resulting sequence of solutions satisfies the conservation laws and possesses certain uniform bounds. Section
4
is devoted to verifying that the renormalized collision terms
(\mathcal{R}_{1})
,
(\mathcal{R}_{2})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})
are well-defined within the appropriate space of distribution functions. Section
5
establishes the strong convergence of the solution sequence
f^{n}
to
f
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. The convergence of the renormalized collision operators
(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}
is then addressed in Section
6
, while Section
7
is devoted to the analysis of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. In Section
8
, we prove that
f
is a renormalized solution to the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) and show that it satisfies the entropy inequality. Finally, in Section
9
we provide a counterexample that there exists a positive function
f(v)
with finite mass, energy, and entropy such that the collision integral diverges for the soft potential case
-3<\gamma<0
.

2.
Toolbox

First, we present some commonly used conclusions in functional analysis, which play a crucial role in verifying convergence later.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a subset of
L^{1}(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
. We say that
\mathscr{F}
is equi-integrable if for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for any measurable set
E\subset X
with
\mu(E)<\delta
and for all
f\in\mathscr{F}
,

\int_{E}|f|dx<\epsilon.

Lemma 2.1

(Dunford-Pettis,
[
16
]
)
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{F}
is a Bounded subset of
L^{1}(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
.
\mathscr{F}
is weakly relatively compact if and only if

(1)

\mathscr{F}
is equi-integrable;

(2)

for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists compact set
K\subset X
such that

\int_{K^{c}}|f(x)|dx<\epsilon,\quad\forall f\in\mathscr{F}.

Lemma 2.2

(Vitali Convergence Theorem,
[
16
,
18
]
)
.

Suppose
1\leq p<\infty
and
\{f^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset L^{p}(X)
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f^{n}-f\|_{L^{p}(X)}=0
if and only if

(1)

f^{n}\to f
in measure;

(2)

\{f^{n}\}
is equi-integrable;

(3)

for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists compact set
K\subset X
such that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{K^{c}}|f^{n}(x)|dx<\epsilon.

In fact, if a sequence converges in measure, there exists a subsequence that converges almost everywhere. In a finite measure space, convergence almost everywhere implies convergence in measure. Consequently, in the case of a finite measure space (where
X
is a compact set), condition (
1
) can be strengthened to the requirement that
f^{n}
converges to
f
almost everywhere. The detailed proof can be found in
[
18
]
.

Lemma 2.3

(Monotone Convergence Theorem,
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\{f^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of measurable functions on
X
and suppose that

(1)

f_{1}(x)\leq f_{2}(x)\leq\cdots\leq f_{n}(x)\leq f_{n+1}(x)\leq\cdots
a.e. on
X
;

(2)

\sup_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}f^{n}(x)dx<\infty
.

Then
f^{n}
converges a.e. on
X
to a finite limit, which we denote by
f
; the function
f
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
and

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}|f^{n}(x)-f(x)|dx=0.

Lemma 2.4

(Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem,
[
9
]
)
.

Suppose that
\{f^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of measurable functions on
X
such that

(1)

f^{n}(x)\rightarrow f(x)
, a.e. on
X
;

(2)

there is a function
g\in L^{1}(X)
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
|f^{n}(x)|\leq g(x)
, a.e. on
X
.

Then
f\in L^{1}(X)
and

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}|f^{n}(x)-f(x)|dx=0.

Lemma 2.5

(Fatou’s lemma,
[
9
]
)
.

If
f^{n}:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is measurable, for each positive integer
n
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}f^{n}(x)dx\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}f^{n}(x)dx

Lemma 2.6

(Egorov’s Theorem,
[
9
]
)
.

If a sequence of measurable functions
f^{n}
converges to a measurable function
f
almost everywhere, then for any
\delta>0
there exists a measurable set
\Omega_{\delta}\subseteq\Omega
such that
\mu(\Omega_{\delta})\geq\mu(\Omega)-\delta
and
f_{n}
converges to
f
uniformly on
\Omega_{\delta}
.

Lemma 2.7

(Product Limit Theorem,
[
8
,
11
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a finite, positive Borel measure on a Borel subset
X
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Assume that
f^{n},f\in L^{1}(d\mu)
and
g^{n},g\in L^{\infty}(d\mu)
satisfy

(1)

w-\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}f^{n}=f
in
L^{1}(d\mu)
;

(2)

\{g^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(d\mu)
and
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}g^{n}=g,\text{a.e.}

Then
w-\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}f^{n}g^{n}=fg
in
L^{1}(d\mu)
, where “
w-\lim
” means weak limit.

Lemma 2.8

(M. Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov Theorem,
[
9
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a bounded subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}),\ 1\leq p<+\infty
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is relatively compact if and only if the following statements hold:

(1)

for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
and any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|h|<\delta
one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}|f(x+h)-f(x)|^{p}dx<\epsilon;

(2)

for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a bounded measurable set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\setminus K}|f(x)|^{p}dx<\epsilon.

Next, we present the weak lower semicontinuity of entropy, See Lemma 2.6 in
[
17
]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Assume that
f^{n}(x,v)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})_{+}
satisfies

(1)

there exists a constant
M>0
such that

\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\leq M,\ f^{n}\log f^{n}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v});

(2)

the sequence
\{f^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
converges to
f
weakly in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
.

Then

(2.1)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f\log fdxdv\leq\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\log f^{n}dxdv.

Lemma 2.10

(Averaged velocity lemma,
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

Let
g^{n}(t,x,v)
converge weakly to
g(t,x,v)
in
L^{1}_{loc}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, and
f^{n}(t,x,v)
converge weakly to
f(t,x,v)
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. Moreover, they satisfy

\tfrac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{n}=g^{n}\quad\text{in}\ \mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}).

Set
\{\phi^{n}(t,x,v)\}_{n\geq 1}\subset L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
converge to
\phi(t,x,v)
a.e.
(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
. Then the sequence
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\phi^{n}dv
converges strongly to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f\phi dv
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})
.

Based on Lemma
2.10
, one can generalize the previous result. Let
(E,\mu)
denote the given measure space.

Lemma 2.11

(Generalized averaged velocity lemma,
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

Suppose that the bounded sequence
\{\phi^{n}(t,x,v,e)\}_{n\geq 1}
in
L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v};L^{1}(E))
converges to a function
\phi\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v};L^{1}(E))
such that the following limit holds

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\phi^{n}-\phi\|_{L^{1}(E)}(t,x,v)=0\text{ a.e. }(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}.

Moreover, assume that
f^{n}(t,x,v)
and
g^{n}(t,x,v)
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
2.10
. It follows that the sequence
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\phi^{n}dv
converges strongly to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f\phi dv
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times E)
.

3.
global existence of approximation equation

In this section, we focus on constructing the following approximate problem, which is given by

(3.1)

\tfrac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{n}=\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})\,,\ f^{n}|_{t=0}=f^{n}_{0}\,,

where
f^{n}_{0}=f^{n}(0,x,v)
denotes the approximate initial data and
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
represents the nonlinear collision operator which will be explicitly constructed later. Subsequently, we establish the existence, positivity and uniqueness of the approximate problem. In addition, we verify the regularity of the solution to equation (
3.1
). Finally, we derive the conservation laws, the entropy inequality, and the uniform-in-
n
bounds associated with the approximate problem (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})_{+}
satisfies (
1.18
), i.e.,

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f_{0}(x,v)(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f_{0}|)dxdv<+\infty.

Then, there exists a sequence
\{f^{n}_{0}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
(where
\mathscr{S}
denotes the Schwartz space over
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
) and a constant
C>0
such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})|f^{n}_{0}-f_{0}|dxdv=0\,,\ f^{n}_{0}\geq\tfrac{1}{n}\mathrm{exp}\left(-\tfrac{|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}}{2}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}_{0}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f^{n}_{0}|)dxdv\leq C\,,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}_{0}\log f^{n}_{0}dxdv=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f_{0}\log f_{0}dxdv\,.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as Lemmas 3.1 and 3.3 of
[
43
]
. We omit the details here for
simplicity.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Consider a smooth cutoff function
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(B_{\frac{4}{3}}\right)
satisfying
0\leq\phi\leq 1
and
\phi\equiv 1
on
B_{\frac{3}{4}}
. We introduce the truncated angular kernel

(3.3)

\displaystyle b_{n}(\cos\theta)=b(\cos\theta)\left[1-\phi\left(n\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right)\right],

and define the collision kernel as

(3.4)

\displaystyle B_{n}(z,\sigma)=|z|^{\gamma}b_{n}(\cos\theta),\quad\text{where }z\cdot\sigma=|z|\cos\theta.

Consequently, the non-negative term
B_{n}(z,\sigma)
fulfills the properties listed below.

(1)

B_{n}(z,\sigma)=0
whenever
\frac{z}{|z|}\cdot\sigma\geq 1-\frac{2}{n},
and the limit
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}B_{n}(z,\sigma)=B(z,\sigma)
holds for all
(z,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
;

(2)

The convergence results
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}M_{n}(|z|)=M(|z|)
and
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}M^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)=M^{\prime}(|z|)
are valid for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z}
. Here
M_{n}(|z|)
is defined as
M(|z|)
with its collision kernel replaced by
B_{n}
, and likewise
M^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)
is defined as
M^{\prime}(|z|)
with its collision kernel replaced by
B_{n}.

Remark 3.1
.

The angular cutoff
b_{n}(\cos\theta)
can be found in
[
30
,
31
]
.

Proof.

Based on the definition of the smooth function
\phi
, we conclude that if
\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\leq\frac{3}{4n}
or equivalently
\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\geq\frac{\sqrt{16n^{2}-9}}{4n}
, then
b_{n}(\cos\theta)=0.
Subsequently, by applying the double-angle formula, we obtain that if

\cos\theta\geq 1-\frac{9}{8n^{2}}\geq 1-\frac{2}{n},\quad b_{n}(\cos\theta)=0.

Furthermore, for any fixed
\theta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
,
n\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\to+\infty
as
n\to\infty
, which implies that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\phi\left(n\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right)=0.

The definition of the approximate kernel
B_{n}
in (
3.4
) implies that whenever
\frac{z}{|z|}\cdot\sigma\geq 1-\frac{2}{n}
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}B_{n}(z,\sigma)=B(z,\sigma),\quad\ \text{ and }\ B_{n}(z,\sigma)\leq B(z,\sigma)\text{ for all }(z,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}.

Thus, by the Lebesgue’s
Dominated Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.4
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}M_{n}(|z|)=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma=M(|z|),\quad\text{ for all }z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z}.

Furthermore, this convergence implies that
M_{n}(|z|)\leq M(|z|)
. We can similarly analyze

M^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma,

where

\displaystyle B_{n}^{\prime}(z,\sigma)=\sup_{1<\lambda\leq\sqrt{2}}\frac{|B_{n}(\lambda z,\sigma)-B_{n}(z,\sigma)|}{(\lambda-1)|z|}=\sup_{1<\lambda\leq\sqrt{2}}\frac{|\lambda z|^{\gamma}-|z|^{\gamma}}{(\lambda-1)|z|}b_{n}(\cos\theta)\leq B^{\prime}(z,\sigma).

This implies that
M^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)\leq M^{\prime}(|z|)
. Applying the Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.4
) again, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}M^{\prime}_{n}(|z|)=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\prime}_{n}(z,\sigma)(1-k\cdot\sigma)d\sigma=M^{\prime}(|z|),\quad\text{ for all }z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{z}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

The above lemmas provide the basis for constructing the approximation equation. Let

(3.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)=\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}fdv\right)^{-1}Q_{n}(f,f),

where

(3.6)

\displaystyle Q_{n}(f,f)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}(v-v_{*},\sigma)(f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}-ff_{*})dv_{*}d\sigma.

It is evident that the approximate collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)
is a quasilinearization of
Q_{n}(f,f)
and admits a linear upper bound in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
as described in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a positive constant
C_{n}
depending on
n
such that for all
f,g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, one has

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\leq C_{n}\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})},

\displaystyle\|\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)-\tilde{Q}_{n}(g,g)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\leq C_{n}\|f-g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}.

Furthermore, if
f\in L^{\infty}\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, one has

(3.8)

\|\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\leq C_{n}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as Lemmas 3.7-3.8 of
[
43
]
. We omit the details here for
simplicity.
∎

Furthermore, by employing arguments similar to those in the Lemma 3.9 of
[
43
]
,
we establish the existence and uniqueness of the non-negative smooth solution
f^{n}
to the approximate problem (
3.1
). Specifically, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4

(Existence, Uniqueness and Positivity)
.

Consider the approximated initial data
f_{0}^{n}
constructed in Lemma
3.1
and the approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f,f)
constructed in (
3.5
). Then the approximated problem (
3.1
) admits a unique distributional solution
f^{n}
satisfying

(1)

for any fixed
T>0
,
f^{n}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
and

f^{n}(t,x,v)\geq\tfrac{1}{n}\mathrm{exp}[-c_{0}(|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})],\quad\textrm{for all }\ (t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v};

(2)

For any multi-index
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{6})\in\mathbb{N}^{7}
with
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|=\sum_{i=0}^{6}\zeta_{i}\leq m
, we have

D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}f^{n}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\cap L^{\infty}((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v};(1+|x|^{k}+|v|^{k})dxdv))\,,

and

\sup_{(t,x,v)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}|D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}f^{n}|+\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(1+|x|^{k}+|v|^{k})|D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}f^{n}|dxdv\leq C\,.

Here the constant
c_{0}=c_{0}(T)>0
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that
f^{n}
is the distributional solution to the approximated problem (
3.1
) constructed in Lemma
3.4
. Then it further enjoys the following properties:

(1)

Mass, momentum, energy and angular momentum are all conserved. Hence, for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(t,\cdot)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\[5.69054pt]
v\\[5.69054pt]
|v|^{2}\\[5.69054pt]
|x-tv|^{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\end{array}\right)dxdv=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f_{0}^{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\[5.69054pt]
v\\[5.69054pt]
|v|^{2}\\[5.69054pt]
|x|^{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\end{array}\right)dxdv;

(2)

The entropy identity holds. Hence for all
t\geq 0
,

(3.9)

\displaystyle H(f^{n}_{0})=H(f^{n})(t)+\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}(\tau,x,v)dxdv,

where the density of entropy dissipation is given by

(3.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{e}^{n}(t,x,v)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}(v-v_{*},\sigma)

\displaystyle\times({f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n}f^{n}_{*})\log\left(\frac{{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}{f^{n}f^{n}_{*}}\right)dv_{*}d\sigma,

and the
H
-functional
H(f)
is defined as in (
1.22
);

(3)

For all
t\geq 0
, we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(t,x,v)(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv\leq

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}_{0}(x,v)(1+2|x|^{2}+(2t^{2}+1)|v|^{2})dxdv,

and

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(t,x,v)|\log f^{n}|dxdv+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{+\infty}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}_{0}(x,v)(|\log f^{n}_{0}|+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv+C_{1},

where the constant
C_{1}>0
is independent of
n
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as Lemma 3.11 of
[
43
]
. We omit the details here for
simplicity.
∎

4.
integrability of the renormalized collision operator

In this paper, we consider

(4.1)

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}(s)=\frac{s}{1+\delta s},\ \text{ for any }\ \delta>0.

Obviously,
\beta_{\delta}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
satisfies condition (
1.20
). Moreover, for any fixed
\delta>0
,
\beta_{\delta}
is bounded. Consequently, for any non-negative functions
f
and
f^{\prime}
, we define

(4.2)

\displaystyle\Gamma(f,f^{\prime})=\beta_{\delta}(f^{\prime})-\beta_{\delta}(f)-\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)(f^{\prime}-f).

Since
\beta_{\delta}
is concave, it follows that
\Gamma(f,f^{\prime})\leq 0
. Following the argument in Section 3 of
[
6
]
, for almost all
(t,x,v)
, the renormalized collision operator
\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)=(\mathcal{R}_{1})+(\mathcal{R}_{2})+(\mathcal{R}_{3}),

where

(4.3)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{1})

\displaystyle=\left[f\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)-\beta_{\delta}(f)\right]\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(f^{\prime}_{*}-f_{*})dv_{*}\,d\sigma\,,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{2})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B\left[f^{\prime}_{*}\beta_{\delta}(f^{\prime})-f_{*}\beta_{\delta}(f)\right]dv_{*}\,d\sigma\,,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{3})

\displaystyle=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}Bf^{\prime}_{*}\Gamma(f,f^{\prime})dv_{*}\,d\sigma\,.

In particular, we define

(4.4)

\mathscr{S}f\equiv\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)(f^{\prime}_{*}-f_{*})dv_{*}\,d\sigma.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{1})=\left[f\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)-\beta_{\delta}(f)\right]\mathscr{S}f.

Remark 4.1
.
