\displaystyle({f^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n})^{2}=\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}.

Combining these relations, we obtain

(7.5)

\displaystyle f^{n}_{*}\frac{({f^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n})^{2}}{(1+f^{n})^{2}(1+{f^{n}}^{\prime})}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}f^{n}_{*}(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}_{*}}\right)^{2}.

Thus, combining (
6.2
) with the condition
\gamma\in[0,1]
, we derive that

(7.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}dv=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}f^{n}_{*}\frac{({f^{n}}^{\prime}-f^{n})^{2}}{(1+f^{n})^{2}(1+{f^{n}}^{\prime})}dvdv_{*}d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}\left(\sqrt{{f^{n}}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\sqrt{{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{f^{n}_{*}}\right)^{2}dvdv_{*}d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}S^{n}(|v-v_{*}|)dvdv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu,R}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})dv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv.

Therefore, the average time-velocity estimate for
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
is established.
∎

Remark 7.1
.

For hard potentials where
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
, the inequality (
7.6
) holds, thereby yielding the desired average time-velocity bound. However, when
-3<\gamma<0
, the above argument only provides the weaker bound

C_{\nu}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\,dv\,dv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\!dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}\,dv.

As shown in Example
9.1
, the integral
\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\,dv\,dv_{*}
may diverge due to the singularity of the collision kernel at small relative velocities. Consequently, the average time-velocity estimate generally fails in the case of soft potentials. Therefore, the approach developed for the hard potential model in this paper does not apply to the soft potential model.

Having established the corresponding bounds, we now investigate the equi-integrability of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
. Given that the function
\beta_{\delta}(s)
satisfies the renormalization condition (
1.20
) and
f^{n}
is the unique solution to the approximate equation (
3.1
) constructed in Section
3
, it follows that
\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})
satisfies

(7.7)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})=\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n}),

where

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})Q_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}\left\{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\right\}.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\right)
with compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
, the sequence
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv
is equi-integrable on
B_{R}(x)
.

Proof.

Following a procedure similar to that in Lemma
4.5
, we multiply both sides of the equation (
7.7
) by a function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\right)
with compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
, and integrate it over
(t,x,v)\in([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. This yields

(7.8)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(T,\cdot)\varphi dxdv-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(0,\cdot)\varphi dxdv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}\left((\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\right)\varphi dxdv,

which implies that

(7.9)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(T,\cdot)+\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(0,\cdot))\varphi dxdv\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}((\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n})\varphi dxdv\right|.

Recall that Sections
6.1
and
6.2
established the weak convergence of the integrals
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}\varphi\,dv
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}\varphi dv
, respectively. Applying the Dunford-Pettis theorem (Lemma
2.1
) confirms that these sequences are equi-integrable on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Furthermore, the bound
\beta_{\delta}(t)\leq t
and the weak convergence of
f^{n}
imply that
\{\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
is also equi-integrable. Thus for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for any measurable set
E\subset B_{R}(x)
with
\mu(E)<\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left|(\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(T,\cdot)+\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(0,\cdot))\varphi\right|dxdv

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left|\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}((\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n})\varphi\right|dxdv<\epsilon.

Thus, the sequence
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv
is equi-integrable on
B_{R}(x)
.
∎

Next, to address the singularity of the collision kernel at
\theta=0
, we investigate the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
by decomposing the spherical integral into two regions: one near the singularity and one away from it. Specifically, we define the truncated kernels as

(7.10)

\displaystyle B_{n}^{k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{k}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}},

\displaystyle B_{k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B^{k}(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{1}{k}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}},

\displaystyle B_{n,k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B_{n}(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\frac{1}{k}},

\displaystyle B_{k}(|z|,\cos\theta)=B(|z|,\cos\theta)\mathbf{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\frac{1}{k}}.

Let
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}
denote the renormalized collision operators corresponding to the kernels
B_{n}^{k}
and
B_{n,k}
, respectively. The operators
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}
are defined analogously. Furthermore, we set

(7.11)

\displaystyle M^{n}(x)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(1+|v|^{2})dv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv,

\displaystyle N^{n}_{L}=\left\{x\in B_{R}(x):M^{n}(x)>L\right\},

\displaystyle M(x)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|v|^{2})dv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv,

\displaystyle N_{L}=\left\{x\in B_{R}(x):M(x)>L\right\}.

Then by Chebyshev’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\text{meas}(N^{n}_{L})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(1+|v|^{2})dxdv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dxdv\leq\frac{C}{L},

\displaystyle\text{meas}(N_{L})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|v|^{2})dxdv+\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edxdv\leq\frac{C}{L}.

With these truncations in place, we first establish the convergence of the non-singular part in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3
.

There exists a sufficiently large constant
L>0
such that

(7.12)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv.

Proof.

The definition of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}
implies that for any fixed
t,x,v
, the operator satisfies the pointwise estimate

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n}^{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)f_{*}^{n}\Gamma(f^{n},{f^{n}}^{\prime})\,dv_{*}d\sigma\leq C(f^{n}+{f^{n}}^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}\,dv_{*}.

Combining this estimate with the convergence
f^{n}\to f
a.e. in
(t,x,v)
and
B^{k}_{n}\to B^{k}
a.e. in
(v_{*},\sigma)
, we obtain the pointwise convergence

(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\to(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\quad\text{a.e. }(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times(N_{L}^{n}\cup N_{L})^{c}\times B_{R}(v).

Furthermore, Lemma
7.1
provides the uniform bound

\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dv\leq C_{L}.

Consequently, by Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.4
), for any
\delta>0
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
such that for all
n>N
, the following inequality holds

\left|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left[\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}-(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\right]\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n}\cup N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv\right|<\frac{\delta}{3}.

Given that
\text{meas}(N_{L})\leq\frac{C}{L}
, and invoking the equi-integrability of the term
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f^{n}\,dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv
on
B_{R}(x)
, we can choose
L>0
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}\cap N_{L}}\,dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}}\,dxdv<\frac{\delta}{3}.

Similarly, we have

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}\cap N_{L}^{n}}\,dxdv<\frac{\delta}{3}.

Therefore, for any
\delta>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
L>0
such that for
n>N
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}\,dxdv-\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left[\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}-(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\right]\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}\cap(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv\right|

\displaystyle+\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}\cap N_{L}}\,dxdv

\displaystyle+\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}\cap N_{L}^{n}}\,dxdv<\delta.

This completes the proof of Lemma
7.3
.
∎

Having established the convergence of the non-singular part in Lemma
7.3
, we now turn to the singular part. We begin by presenting an estimate that plays a crucial role in proving the uniform convergence of the singular integrals.

Lemma 7.4

(Uniform smallness estimate for small angular deflections
\theta
)
.

Let
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
,
\nu\in[0,1/2]
defined in (
1.8
), and
R>0
. For the collision kernel
B_{k}
defined in (
7.10
), the following estimate holds

(7.13)

\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma dv\leq\frac{C}{k}(1+|v_{*}|)^{1+\gamma+\nu}\|g\|^{2}_{{H^{\nu/2}}{(B_{R}(v))}}

for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

We fix
(v,v_{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}
and denote the relative velocity by
u=v-v_{*}
. The scattering angle
\theta\in(0,1/k]
is defined by the relation
u\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta|u|
.
Recalling the post-collisional velocity formula
v^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma
, it follows immediately that the velocity change
h:=v^{\prime}-v
can be expressed as

h=\frac{v_{*}-v}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma=\frac{|u|}{2}\left(\sigma-\frac{u}{|u|}\right).

From this representation, we obtain
|h|=|u|\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)
. We now turn to a more detailed decomposition of
h
. To this end, we decompose the unit vector
\sigma
into its components parallel and orthogonal to
u
, namely,

\sigma=\cos\theta\frac{u}{|u|}+\sin\theta\,\omega,

where
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
satisfies
\omega\perp u
. Substituting this decomposition into the expression for
h
, we obtain

h=\frac{|u|}{2}\left((\cos\theta-1)\frac{u}{|u|}+\sin\theta\,\omega\right).

Accordingly, it is natural to decompose
h
into a component parallel
u
to and a component perpendicular to
u
:

h=h_{1}+h_{2},

where

h_{1}=\frac{|u|}{2}(\cos\theta-1)\frac{u}{|u|}=-u\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right),

and

h_{2}=\frac{|u|}{2}\sin\theta\,\omega=|u|\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\,\omega.

In particular, this decomposition implies that
|h_{2}|=\frac{1}{2}|u|\sin\theta
.
Moreover, since
\theta\in[0,1/k]
, we can use the inequality
\sin\theta\leq\theta
to conclude that

|h_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{2k}|u|
. Next, we introduce the radial variable
r:=|h_{2}|
. In the plane orthogonal to
u
, the corresponding measure element takes the form
dh_{2}=r\,dr\,d\phi
, where
\phi
denotes the azimuthal angle. At the same time, the above relations allow us to rewrite the components of
\sigma
in terms of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
as

\sin\theta\,\omega=\frac{2}{|u|}h_{2},\quad\cos\theta\frac{u}{|u|}=\frac{2}{|u|}h_{1}+\frac{u}{|u|}.

We now proceed to the change of variables from the spherical measure
d\sigma
to the perpendicular velocity variable
h_{2}
. Recalling that

\displaystyle d\sigma=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi\quad\text{ and }r=\frac{|u|}{2}\sin\theta,

we deduce that

dr=\frac{|u|}{2}\cos\theta\,d\theta,\quad d\theta=\frac{2}{|u|\cos\theta}dr.

Furthermore, using the identities
\sin\theta=\frac{2r}{|u|},
and
\cos\theta=\sqrt{1-\frac{4r^{2}}{|u|^{2}}}
,
we can rewrite the surface measure
d\sigma
in terms of
r
, and obtain

\displaystyle d\sigma=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi=\frac{4}{|u|^{2}}\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-\frac{4r^{2}}{|u|^{2}}}}drd\phi.

Finally, observing that
dh_{2}=rdrd\phi
, we conclude that the Jacobian of the transformation is given by

d\sigma=\frac{4}{|u|^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{4}{|u|^{2}}r^{2}}}\,dh_{2}.

Under the singular kernel assumption

\displaystyle b(\cos\theta)\sim K\theta^{-2-\nu},\quad\text{as }\theta\to 0^{+},

we note that, for grazing collisions,
|h_{2}|\approx\frac{1}{2}|u|\theta
.
Consequently,
as\theta\to 0^{+}
, the kernel admits the asymptotic representation

b(\cos\theta)\sim K\theta^{-2-\nu}\sim\left(\frac{|h_{2}|}{|u|}\right)^{-2-\nu}.

Let
r_{k}:=\frac{1}{2}|u|\sin\frac{1}{k}
. We now estimate the collision integral associated with
B_{k}
.
Using the above asymptotic behavior together with the change of variables established previously, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{K,\nu}\int_{|h_{2}|<r_{k}}|u|^{\gamma}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h_{2}|^{2+\nu}}\frac{|u|^{2+\nu}}{|u|^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{4r^{2}}{|u|^{2}}}}\,dh_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{K,\nu}\int_{|h_{2}|<r_{k}}|u|^{\gamma+\nu}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h|^{3+\nu}}\cdot\frac{|h|^{3+\nu}}{|h_{2}|^{2+\nu}}\,dh_{2}.

Here, we observe the following geometric bound for the ratio of the norms

\frac{|h|^{3+\nu}}{|h_{2}|^{2+\nu}}=\frac{\left(|u|\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{3+\nu}}{\left(|u|\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2+\nu}}\leq C|u|\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\leq C|u|\frac{1}{k},

where we have used the restriction
\theta\in(0,1/k]
. Substituting this bound into the previous estimate yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}C^{\prime\prime}_{K,\nu}|u|^{1+\gamma+\nu}\int_{|h_{2}|<r_{k}}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h|^{3+\nu}}\,dh_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\tilde{C}_{K,\nu}|u|^{1+\gamma+\nu}\int_{|h|<\sqrt{2}r_{k}}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h|^{3+\nu}}\,dh.

Given that
|v|\leq R
, we have the estimate
|u|^{1+\gamma+\nu}\leq C_{R}(1+|v_{*}|^{1+\gamma+\nu})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma dv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}C_{K,\nu,R}\left(1+|v_{*}|^{1+\gamma+\nu}\right)\int_{B_{R}(v)}dv\int_{|h|\leq\sqrt{2}r_{k}}\frac{(g(v+h)-g(v))^{2}}{|h|^{3+\nu}}\,dh.

Finally, recalling from Section 7.3 of
[
26
]
that the Sobolev norm admits the equivalent characterization

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}^{2}\approx\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}^{2}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v^{\prime}}}\frac{(f(v^{\prime})-f(v))^{2}}{|v^{\prime}-v|^{3+2s}}\mathbf{1}_{|v-v^{\prime}|\leq 1}\,dvdv^{\prime},

we conclude that

\displaystyle\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{k}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g(v^{\prime})-g(v)\right)^{2}\,d\sigma dv\leq\frac{1}{k}C_{K,\nu,R}\left(1+|v_{*}|^{1+\gamma+\nu}\right)\|g\|_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}^{2}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
7.4
.
∎

Finally, combining the convergence of the non-singular part with the estimates for the singular part, we obtain the convergence of
(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\right)
with compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
, we have

(7.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})\varphi dxdv.

Proof.

Analogous to the estimate in (
7.6
), we bound
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\,dv\leq\frac{1}{2}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n,k}f_{*}^{n}\left(\sqrt{f^{n\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\varphi\,dv\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma.

Recalling the parameters
\gamma
and
\nu
defined in (
1.7
) and (
1.8
), we find that

\displaystyle 1+\gamma+\nu=1+1-\frac{4}{s-1}+\frac{2}{s-1}=2-\frac{2}{s-1}\leq 2.

Combining this inequality with Lemma
7.4
leads to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\,dv

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}dv_{*}\iint_{B_{R}(v)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{n,k}\left(\sqrt{f^{n\prime}}-\sqrt{f^{n}}\right)^{2}\varphi\,dvd\sigma

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}C_{k,v,R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}(1+|v_{*}|^{1+\gamma+\nu})\,dv_{*}\cdot\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|^{2}_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}C_{k,v,R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})\,dv_{*}\cdot\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|^{2}_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}.

Moreover, as shown in
[
5
]
, estimate (
5.3
) provides a fractional Sobolev control in the velocity variable, which implies that

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|^{2}_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}\leq\frac{C_{0}J^{2}(2\pi)^{3}}{\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\left(r_{0}/2\right)^{\gamma}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}(t,x,v)\,dv+\left(C_{\nu}+\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}_{0}}\right)\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\right\}.

Recalling the definition in (
5.12
), the set
W_{\epsilon}
is given by

\displaystyle W_{\epsilon}=\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times B_{R}(x):\int_{B_{R}(v)}g^{2}dv>\epsilon\right\},

where
g(t,x,v)
denotes the weak limit of
\sqrt{f^{n}}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(x))
. Due to the strong convergence of
f^{n}
, we identify
g=\sqrt{f}
almost everywhere in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(x)
. Furthermore, by Lemma
5.5
, there exists a Borel set
U_{\delta}
with measure less than
\delta
such that for sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the lower bound
\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\,dv\geq\epsilon/2
holds on
W_{\epsilon}\setminus U_{\delta}
. Accordingly, we can find an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n>N
, we have

\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\geq\epsilon/2
on
W_{\epsilon}\setminus U_{\delta}
and
\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\leq 2\epsilon\
a.e. on
([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}
.
Hence, for any fixed
L>0
and any
n>N
, the integral over the set
W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}C_{k,v,R}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{B_{R}(x)}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f_{*}^{n}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})\,dv_{*}\cdot\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|^{2}_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{L}{k}C_{k,v,R}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{B_{R}(x)}\|\sqrt{f^{n}}\|^{2}_{H^{\nu/2}(B_{R}(v))}\mathbf{1}_{W_{\epsilon}\setminus(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{L}{\epsilon k}\tilde{C}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dxdv+\bigg(C_{\nu}+\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}_{0}}\bigg)\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(1+|v|^{2})dxdv\bigg)\to 0\quad(k\to\infty).

Here
\tilde{C}=C_{k,v,R}C_{0}J^{2}(2\pi)^{3}(r_{0}/2)^{-\gamma}
.
The second inequality follows from the definition of the set
(U^{n}_{L})^{c}
, while the last step is derived from the uniform lower bound of
\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\,dv
on
W_{\epsilon}\setminus U_{\delta}
combined with the estimate (
5.3
).

Furthermore, invoking the measure bound
\text{meas}(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})\leq\delta+\frac{C}{L}
and the equi-integrability of
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f^{n}\,dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv
on
B_{R}(x)
estiblished in Lemma
7.2
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(U_{\delta}\cup U_{L}^{n})}\,dxdv\to 0

as
\delta\to 0
and
L\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, we conclude that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{W_{\epsilon}}\,dxdv=0.

Furthermore, invoking Lemma
7.1
yields the estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}}\,dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu,R}\sup_{n>N}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}_{*}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})dxdv_{*}\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\mathbf{1}_{([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}}dv+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\epsilon C_{\nu,R}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}_{*}(1+|v_{*}|^{2})dxdv_{*}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{n}dxdv\to 0\quad(\epsilon\to 0).

Here, the last inequality follows from the bound
\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\leq 2\epsilon\
, which is valid a.e. on
([0,T]\times B_{R}(x))\setminus W_{\epsilon}
. Combining these convergence results, we obtain

(7.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n>N}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}\varphi\,dxdv=0.

On the other hand, for any fixed
n
with
1\leq n\leq N
and an arbitrary
\delta>0
, there exists a threshold
k_{n}>0
such that for all
k\geq k_{n}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}\varphi\,dxdv<\delta.

By selecting a uniform threshold
\tilde{k}=\max\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{N}\}
, we ensure that the estimate holds simultaneously for all
1\leq n\leq N
whenever
k>\tilde{k}
. Consequently, given any
\delta>0
and any
k>\tilde{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}\varphi dxdv<\delta,\quad\forall 1\leq n\leq N.

This establishes the desired convergence

(7.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}_{k}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\bigg)\varphi dxdv=0,\quad\forall 1\leq n\leq N.

Thus, combining the convergence results (
7.15
) and (
7.16
), for sufficiently large
L>0
, we establish the convergence

(7.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\bigg)\varphi dxdv=0.

Furthermore, the measure bound
\text{meas}(N_{L}^{n})\leq C/L
, combined with the equi-integrability of the sequence
\int_{0}^{T}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\big)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dv
on
B_{R}(x)
, implies that

(7.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\,\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}^{n}}\,dx\,dv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi\,\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}^{n}}\,dx\,dv\to 0\quad(L\to\infty).

Similarly, we have

(7.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\,\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}}\,dx\,dv=0.

By Lemma
7.3
, the convergence for the angular cutoff case satisfies

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dxdv

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv.

Combining this result with the convergences established in (
7.17
)-(
7.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\varphi dxdv-\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})\varphi dxdv\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L}^{n})^{c}}dxdv-\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\varphi\mathbf{1}_{(N_{L})^{c}}\,dxdv\bigg|

\displaystyle+\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n,k}\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}^{n}}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{k}\mathbf{1}_{N_{L}}\bigg)\varphi\,dx\,dv

\displaystyle+\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}\,dv\bigg)^{-1}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{k}\bigg)\varphi\,dxdv\to 0

as
n\to\infty,k\to\infty
and
L\to\infty
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.5
.
∎

8.
Existence and entropy inequality

This section is devoted to completing the proof of Theorem
1.1
. By Lemma
3.4
, the approximating equation (
3.1
) admits a unique distributional solution
f^{n}
. Moreover, we have shown that the sequence
\{f^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
converges strongly in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
to a limit function
f
. Accordingly, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is divided into two parts. In the first part, we show that the limit function
f
is a renormalized solution of the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
). We then verify that
f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\right))\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv)
, and that
f
satisfies the a priori bound (
1.23
). In the second part, we prove that
f
satisfies the entropy inequality associated with the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
).

8.1.
Existence and continuity of time : Part
\mathrm{I}
of Theorem

Since
f^{n}
is the unique distributional solution to the approximating equation (
3.1
), Definition
1.1
of renormalized solutions implies

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})=\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n}),

where

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f^{n})Q_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}\left\{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\right\}.

Multiplying both sides of the above equation by a test function
\varphi(t,x,v)\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
with compact support in
[0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
and integrating over
(t,x,v)\in([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(T,\cdot)\varphi dxdv-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})(0,\cdot)\varphi dxdv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}\left((\mathcal{R}_{1})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{n}+(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{n}\right)\varphi dxdv.

Taking the limit as
n\to\infty
on both sides, and noting that

f^{n}
converges strongly to
f
and
\beta_{\delta}\in C^{2}
, we have

\frac{\partial\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})}{\partial t}\to\frac{\partial\beta_{\delta}(f)}{\partial t}\quad\text{in }\ \mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}).

By combining this with the convergence properties established in (
6.1
), (
6.5
) and (
7.14
), we obtain

(8.1)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\beta_{\delta}(f)(T,\cdot)-\beta_{\delta}(f)(0,\cdot))\varphi dxdv=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left\{(\mathcal{R}_{1})+(\mathcal{R}_{2})+(\mathcal{R}_{3})\right\}\varphi dxdv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}dt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)\varphi dxdv.

This demonstrates that
f
is actually a renormalized solution of (
1.1
). Furthermore, we find that for any
\Phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};C^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
and any
0\leq s<t<\infty
, the following equality holds

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}(\beta_{\delta}(f)(t,\cdot)-\beta_{\delta}(f)(s,\cdot))\Phi dxdv=

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}d\tau\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(f)Q(f,f)\Phi dxdv.

Invoking the absolute continuity of the integral, we deduce that

\beta_{\delta}(f)\in C\left(\mathbb{R}_{+};\ w\text{-}L^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\right)\right)_{+}.

Additionally, observing that
\beta_{\delta}(s)=s/(1+\delta s)
satisfies

\displaystyle 0\leq s-\beta_{\delta}(s)\leq\epsilon_{K}(\delta)s+1_{s\geq K}s\quad\text{ on }\ [0,\infty),

where
\epsilon_{K}(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0_{+}
, we apply the absolute continuity of the integral again to show that for any
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\|f(t,\cdot)-\beta_{\delta}(f)(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\leq\epsilon_{K}(\delta)\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f1_{f\geq K}dxdv\to 0

as
\delta\to 0_{+}
and
K\to\infty
. Consequently, we conclude that
f\in C\left(\mathbb{R}_{+};w\text{-}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x};B^{-\alpha}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))\right)_{+}
.

Next, we establish the bound (
1.23
), which is given by

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(t,\cdot)(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)dxdv\leq C_{T}.

First, for any
R>0
and
t\in[0,T]
, we observe that

\varphi_{R}\triangleq\left(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}\right)\mathbf{1}_{|x|\leq R}\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}).

Recall that
f^{n}(t)
converges weakly to
f(t)
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Then, Lemma
3.5
implies that there exists a constant
C_{T}>0
, independent of
R
, such that

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(t,x,v)\varphi_{R}dxdv=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(t,x,v)\varphi_{R}dxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{n\geq 1}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv\leq C_{T}\,.

Taking the limit as
R\to+\infty
, we obtain by the Monotone Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.3
) that

(8.2)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(1+|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv\leq C_{T}.

Moreover, Lemmas
2.9
and
3.5
imply that for
t\in[0,T]
, the
H
-functional satisfies

H(f)\leq\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}H(f^{n})\leq C_{T}^{\prime}.

Using the fact that
t\log\frac{1}{t}\leq C_{0}\sqrt{t}
for
t\in(0,1)
with some
C_{0}\geq 0
, we infer that

\displaystyle-\iint_{0<f\leq 1}f\log fdxdv=

\displaystyle-\iint_{0<f\leq e^{-|x|^{2}-|v|^{2}}}f\log fdxdv-\iint_{e^{-|x|^{2}-|v|^{2}}<f\leq 1}f\log fdxdv

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}+|v|^{2}}{2}}dxdv+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})dxdv\,.

Consequently, we have

(8.3)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f|\log f|dxdv=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f\log fdxdv-2\iint_{0<f\leq 1}f\log fdxdv\leq C_{T}.

By combining (
8.2
) and (
8.3
), we conclude that the bound (
1.23
) holds.

8.2.
Entropy inequality: Part
\mathrm{II}
of Theorem

In this section, we focus on proving Part
\mathrm{II}
of Theorem
1.1
. Specifically, we show that the renormalized solution
f
of (
1.1
) satisfies the entropy inequality (
1.24
) given by

(8.4)

H(f)(t)+\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}ds\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B({f}^{\prime}{f^{\prime}_{*}}-ff_{*})\log\left(\frac{{f}^{\prime}{f^{\prime}_{*}}}{ff_{*}}\right)dxdvdv_{*}d\sigma\leq H(f_{0}).

As stated in (
3.9
) of Lemma
3.5
, the approximate distributional solution
f^{n}
to the approximating problem (
3.1
) admits the entropy identity. Consequently, for all
t\geq 0
, we have

H(f^{n}_{0})=H(f^{n})(t)+\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}(\tau,x,v)dxdv,

where
\tilde{e}^{n}
and
H(f^{n})
are defined in (
3.10
) and (
1.22
), respectively. Lemma
3.1
implies that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}H(f^{n}_{0})=H(f_{0})
. In order to prove the entropy inequality (
1.24
) (or (
8.4
)), it suffices to show that for almost all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
, we have

(8.5)

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv.

For
R\in(1,\infty)
, we define

\displaystyle E_{R}=B_{R}(v)\times B_{R}(v_{*})\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\quad d\Theta=dvdv_{*}d\sigma,\quad N(f)=1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}fdv

\displaystyle L^{R}(f)=\iint_{B_{R}(v_{*})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{*}B^{R}(v-v_{*},\sigma)dv_{*}d\sigma,\quad L^{R}_{n}(f)=\iint_{B_{R}(v_{*})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}f_{*}B^{R}_{n}(v-v_{*},\sigma)dv_{*}d\sigma,

where

\displaystyle B^{R}(|z|,\cos\theta)=\left(B(|z|,\cos\theta)1_{\frac{1}{R}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}}\right)\wedge R,\quad B^{R}_{n}(|z|,\cos\theta)=(B_{n}(|z|,\cos\theta)1_{\frac{1}{R}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}})\wedge R.

Since
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{R}_{n}(v-v_{*},\sigma)d\sigma
forms a bounded sequence in
L^{\infty}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v_{*});L^{1}(B_{R}(v)))
, we deduce the strong convergence of
L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})
by applying the generalized averaged velocity lemma (Lemma
2.11
). For further details, we refer to Section 4 of Reference
[
11
]
. More precisely, we have

(8.6)

L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})\to L^{R}(f)\quad\text{ in }L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)).

Lemma 8.1
.

For almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}(x)
, the sequences
\{{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
are relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta)
.

Proof.

We first take an increasing function
\Psi\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+})
satisfying

\displaystyle\Psi(t)\rightarrow+\infty\,\text{as }t\rightarrow+\infty,\quad\Psi(t)(\log t)^{-1}\rightarrow 0\,\text{as }t\rightarrow+\infty.

This implies that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a sufficiently large constant
M
such that
\Psi(t)\leq\epsilon\log t
for all
t\geq M
. Accordingly, we observe that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\Psi(f^{n})dv-\int_{B_{R}(v)}f\Psi(f)dv\right\|_{L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{B_{R}(v)}\left(f^{n}\Psi(f^{n})\mathbf{1}_{f^{n}\leq M}-f\Psi(f)\mathbf{1}_{f\leq M}\right)dv\right\|_{L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\sup_{n\geq 1}\left\|\int_{B_{R}(v)}\left(f^{n}|\log f^{n}|+f|\log f|\right)dv\right\|_{L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))}.

By combining the boundedness of the continuous function
\Psi(t)
on
[0,M]
with the bound of
f^{n}\log f^{n}
in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}))
and the convergence in (
5.10
), we conclude that the inequality above tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
. This implies that the sequence
\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\Psi(f^{n})dv
is compact in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))
. Thus, we deduce that

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}\int_{E_{R}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}

\displaystyle(\Psi(f^{n})+\Psi(f_{*}^{n}))B^{R}_{n}d\Theta

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}\int_{E_{R}}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\Psi({f^{n}}^{\prime})+\Psi({f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}))B^{R}_{n}d\Theta

are compact in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))
.

We claim that if a sequence
f^{n}(x)\geq 0
is compact in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, where
\Omega
is a bounded set, then there exists a function
g(x)\in L^{1}(\Omega)
such that
0\leq f^{n}(x)\leq g(x)
a.e. in
\Omega
(up to a subsequence). Given the compactness of
f^{n}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, there exists a function
f\in L^{1}(\Omega)
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f^{n}-f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=0
. We extract a subsequence such that
\|f^{n+1}-f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and define
g(x)
by

g(x)=f^{1}(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|f^{n+1}(x)-f^{n}(x)|.

By the Monotone Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.3
),
g
belongs to
L^{1}(\Omega)
because

\int_{\Omega}g(x)dx\leq\|f^{1}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}=\|f^{1}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+1<\infty.

Using the telescoping sum representation
f^{m}(x)=f^{1}(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}(f^{n+1}(x)-f^{n}(x))
and the triangle inequality, we obtain
0\leq f^{n}(x)\leq g(x)
for almost every
x\in\Omega
. This establishes the claim. Consequently, there exist non-negative functions
\bar{K},\bar{N}\in L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x))
, independent of
n
, satisfying

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}\int_{E_{R}}f^{n}f_{*}^{n}

\displaystyle(\Psi(f^{n})+\Psi(f_{*}^{n}))B^{R}_{n}d\Theta\leq\bar{K},

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}\int_{E_{R}}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\Psi({f^{n}}^{\prime})+\Psi({f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}))B^{R}_{n}d\Theta\leq\bar{K},

\displaystyle N(f^{n})

\displaystyle\leq\bar{N}.

In addition, for all
M>0
and any Borel subset
A\subset E_{R}
, it follows that for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{A}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}d\Theta\leq

\displaystyle RM^{2}\Theta(A)+\int_{E_{R}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}(\mathbf{1}_{f^{n}\geq M}+\mathbf{1}_{f_{*}^{n}\geq M})B^{R}_{n}d\Theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle RM^{2}\Theta(A)+\Psi(M)^{-1}\bar{K}\bar{N}.

This implies that the sequence
\{f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is equi-integrable, and thus, by Dunford-Pettis theorem (Lemma
2.1
), it is relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta)
for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}
.
By employing analogous arguments, we conclude that
\{{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is also relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta)
for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}(x)
. Finally, the proof of Lemma
8.1
is finished.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(E_{R})
, we establish

(8.7)

\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\varphi d\Theta\to\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}ff_{*}B^{R}\varphi d\Theta\quad\text{strongly in }L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)),

and

(8.8)

\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\varphi d\Theta\rightarrow\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}{f}^{\prime}f_{*}^{\prime}B^{R}\varphi d\Theta\quad\text{strongly in }L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)).

Proof.

Combining (
8.6
) with the strong convergence of
f^{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})\to N(f)^{-1}L^{R}(f)\quad\text{a.e.}\ t,x\in(0,R)\times B_{R}(x).

Furthermore, by invoking Egorov’s Theorem (Lemma
2.6
), for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a Borel
E\subset\Omega_{R}=(0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v)
such that
|E^{c}\cap\Omega_{R}|<\epsilon
and
N(f^{n})^{-1}L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})
converges uniformly to
N(f)^{-1}L^{R}(f)
on
E
. Additionally, we observe the bound

\displaystyle 0\leq N(f^{n})^{-1}L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})\leq\|B^{R}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(v);L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))}.

With these preliminaries, we proceed to prove the convergence (
8.7
).
We first observe the following decomposition

\displaystyle\left\{f^{n}L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})N(f^{n})^{-1}-fL^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\right\}\varphi=

\displaystyle\left\{L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})N(f^{n})^{-1}-L^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\right\}f^{n}\varphi

\displaystyle+\left(f^{n}-f\right)L^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\varphi.

This yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}dt\int_{B_{R}(x)}dx\left|\int_{E_{R}}\left\{f^{n}L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})N(f^{n})^{-1}-fL^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\right\}\varphi d\Theta\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))}\sup\limits_{E}\left|L^{R}_{n}(f^{n})N(f^{n})^{-1}-L^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\right|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\sup_{n\geq 1}\|B^{R}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(v);L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))}+\|B^{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(v);L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))}\right)\left(\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{E^{c}}f^{n}dtdxdv+\int_{E^{c}}fdtdxdv\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}dt\int_{B_{R}(x)}dx|\int_{B_{R}(v)}f^{n}\tilde{\varphi}dv-\int_{B_{R}(v)}f\tilde{\varphi}dv|,

where
\tilde{\varphi}=\mathbf{1}_{E}L^{R}(f)N(f)^{-1}\varphi\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))
.
Since

\sup_{n\geq 1}\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))}\leq C_{R}

by Lemma
3.5
, we conclude that the first quantity on the right-hand side of the above inequality converges to 0 as
n\to+\infty
. Moreover, the convergence in (
5.10
) shows that the last term on the right-hand side of the above inequality converges to 0 as
n\to+\infty
. Furthermore, we have
\sup_{n\geq 1}\|B^{R}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(v);L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))}+\|B^{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R}(v);L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))}\leq 2R
and
f^{n}
converges weakly to
f
in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R}(x)\times B_{R}(v))
, which implies that the second term on the right-hand side of the above inequality vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
. Thus, the assertion (
8.7
) holds.

To establish the convergence (
8.8
), we first observe the identity

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\varphi d\Theta=

\displaystyle\iiint_{B_{R}(v)\times B_{R}(v_{*})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}N(f^{n})^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\varphi^{\prime}d\sigma dvdv_{*},

\displaystyle\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}{f}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{\prime}}B^{R}\varphi d\Theta=

\displaystyle\iiint_{B_{R}(v)\times B_{R}(v_{*})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}N(f)^{-1}ff_{*}B^{R}\varphi^{\prime}d\sigma dvdv_{*},

where
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi(v^{\prime})
.
By employing arguments analogous to those used for (
8.7
), we deduce that the convergence (
8.8
) holds. This completes the proof of Lemma
8.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

For almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}
, we obtain

(8.9)

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow{f}{f_{*}}B^{R},\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow\ f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}B^{R}

weakly in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta).

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we define

\displaystyle P^{n}=N(f^{n})^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle P=N(f)^{-1}{f}{f_{*}}B^{R}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{n}=N(f^{n})^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{P}=N(f)^{-1}f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}B^{R}\,.

The strong convergence of
\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}\varphi d\Theta
implies the existence of a subsequence converging pointwise a.e. in
(t,x)
to
\int_{E_{R}}P\varphi d\Theta
. However, it is important to note that this subsequence may depend on the choice of
\varphi
.
Indeed, for any Borel subset
A
of
E_{R}
, there exists a sequence of measurable subsets
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that
\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}\to\mathbf{1}_{A}
in
L^{1}(E_{R})
. By choosing
\varphi=\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}
and invoking the strong convergence of
\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}d\Theta
, we can extract a subsequence (still denoted by
\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}d\Theta
) such that

\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}d\Theta\to\int_{E_{R}}P\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}d\Theta\quad\text{a.e.}\ (t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}.

Thus, for any Borel subset
A
of
E_{R}
and any constant
M>0
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}(\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}-\mathbf{1}_{A})d\Theta\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{2}\int_{E_{R}}|\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}-\mathbf{1}_{A}|d\Theta+\int_{E_{R}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}(\mathbf{1}_{f^{n}\geq M}+\mathbf{1}_{f_{*}^{n}\geq M})B^{R}_{n}d\Theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{2}\int_{E_{R}}|\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}-\mathbf{1}_{A}|d\Theta+\Psi(M)^{-1}\bar{K}\bar{N}\to 0\quad(k\to\infty,\ M\to\infty).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}\mathbf{1}_{A}d\Theta-\int_{E_{R}}P\mathbf{1}_{A}d\Theta\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{E_{R}}P^{n}(\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}-\mathbf{1}_{A})d\Theta\right|+\left|\int_{E_{R}}(P^{n}-P)\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}d\Theta\right|+\left|\int_{E_{R}}P(\mathbf{1}_{A_{k}}-\mathbf{1}_{A})d\Theta\right|

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0\quad(k\to\infty,\ n\to\infty).

Since the above holds for any measurable subset
A
, it follows that almost every
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}
and for all
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(E_{R})
,

\displaystyle N(f^{n})^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow N(f)^{-1}{f}{f_{*}}B^{R},\quad N(f^{n})^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n}\rightarrow N(f)^{-1}f^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{*}B^{R}

weakly in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta)
. Moreover, using the fact that
N(f^{n})(t,x)
and
\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\right)^{-1}
converge a.e. to
N(f)
and
1
respectively (see (
5.10
)), it follows that (
8.9
) holds.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

The convergence (
8.5
) holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}edv.

Proof.

We first define the function

j(a,b)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}(a-b)\log\frac{a}{b},&\quad\text{for }a,b>0\,,\\
+\infty,&\quad\text{for }a\text{ or }b\leq 0\,,\end{array}\right.

whose Hessian matrix

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{ab}\begin{pmatrix}b+\frac{b^{2}}{a}&-(a+b)\\
-(a+b)&a+\frac{a^{2}}{b}\end{pmatrix},\quad a,b>0

is non-negative. This implies that
j(a,b)
is convex function. Next, we further consider the integral

(8.10)

J(F,G)=\int_{E_{R}}j(F,G)d\Theta,\quad F,\,G\in L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta).

The convexity of
j(a,b)
implies that

\displaystyle j(F^{n},G^{n})\geq j(a,b)+\bigg(\log\frac{a}{b}+\frac{a-b}{b}\bigg)(F^{n}-a)+\bigg(\log\frac{b}{a}+\frac{b-a}{b}\bigg)(G^{n}-b)

for
F^{n},G^{n},a,b>0
. We assume that
F^{n}
and
G^{n}
converge weakly to
F\geq 0
and
G\geq 0
respectively in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta)
. For any
\epsilon>0
and
M>0
, we set
a=\epsilon+F\wedge M
and
b=\epsilon+G\wedge M
. It follows from the convexity of
j(a,b)
that

\displaystyle\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}J(F^{n},G^{n})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(a,b)+(\log\tfrac{a}{b}+\tfrac{a-b}{b})(F-a)+(\log\tfrac{b}{a}+\tfrac{b-a}{b})(G-b)d\Theta

\displaystyle\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(a,b)+(\log\tfrac{a}{b}+\tfrac{a-b}{b})(F\wedge M-a)+(\log\tfrac{b}{a}+\tfrac{b-a}{b})(G\wedge M-b)d\Theta

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{R}}j(a,b)d\Theta-\epsilon\int_{E_{R}}(a-b)(\tfrac{1}{a}-\tfrac{1}{b})d\Theta\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(a,b)d\Theta.

Hence, for any
\epsilon>0
and
M>0
,

\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{E_{R}}j(F^{n},G^{n})d\Theta\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(\epsilon+F\wedge M,\epsilon+G\wedge M)d\Theta.

By letting
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
and
M\to+\infty
, we conclude from Fatou’s lemma (Lemma
2.5
) that

(8.11)

\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{E_{R}}j(F^{n},G^{n})d\Theta\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(F,G)d\Theta.

We now denote by
e^{R}
the functions obtained from
e
by replacing the collision kernel
B
with
B_{R}
, and similarly for
\tilde{e}^{n,R}
. By taking

F^{n}=\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}{f^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{n}}^{\prime}B^{R}_{n},\quad G^{n}=\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f^{n}dv\bigg)^{-1}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}B^{R}_{n},

one has

(8.12)

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n,R}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{R}dv.\quad\text{a.e.}\kern 5.0pt(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}.

Given that
\tilde{e}^{n,R}\leq\tilde{e}^{n}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n}dv\geq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\tilde{e}^{n,R}dv\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}e^{R}dv.

By taking the limit as
R\to+\infty
and applying the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem (Lemma
2.4
), we conclude that the convergence (
8.5
) holds.
∎

The entropy inequality (
1.24
) follows immediately from the above lemma. Finally, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is therefore finished.

9.
On soft potential model
-3<\gamma<0

For the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation, the angular singularity of the collision kernel prevents us from establishing an Arkeryd-type inequality analogous to the one in
[
3
]
. Consequently, our analysis relies primarily on a priori estimates derived from entropy dissipation. For hard potentials, the bound established by (
5.3
) is sufficient to ensure the strong convergence of
f^{n}
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})
. However, for soft potentials, the upper bound in (
5.3
) takes the following form

\frac{C_{0}(2\pi)^{3}}{\|f^{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(v))}\left(2\sqrt{2}R\right)^{\gamma}}\bigg\{\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\,dv\,dv_{*}+\|e^{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}+C_{1}\|f^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})}\bigg\}.

Even if we assume that
f
satisfies the fundamental conservation laws and entropy bounds, specifically
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\cap L\log L)
, this function space, despite its control over global moments and entropy, still permits a certain degree of local concentration. When such concentration combines with the singularity of the soft potential kernel
|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}
(where
-3<\gamma<0
), the collision integral term may diverge to infinity. In other words, for soft potentials, finite entropy is insufficient to guarantee the finiteness of the collision integral
\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f^{n}f^{n}_{*}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\,dv\,dv_{*}
. We will demonstrate this fact by constructing a counterexample.

Example 9.1
.

Let the positive function be defined as

\displaystyle f(v)=\begin{cases}|v|^{-q},&|v|\leq 1\\
e^{-|v|+1},&|v|>1\end{cases},

where
\frac{\gamma+6}{2}<q<3
with
\gamma\in(-3,0)
. Then the function
f(v)
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(v)(1+|v|^{2}+|\log f|)dv<\infty,

but

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f(v)f(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}=+\infty.

Proof.

The proof will be divided into three steps.

Step 1:

Finite mass and energy.
First, by utilizing the condition
q<3
, we can demonstrate that the distribution function
f
possesses finite mass and energy. To be specific,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f(v)dv

\displaystyle=\int_{|v|\leq 1}|v|^{-q}dv+\int_{|v|>1}e^{-|v|+1}dv

\displaystyle=4\pi\int_{0}^{1}r^{2-q}dr+4\pi e\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{2}e^{-r}dr

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{3-q}+20\pi<\infty,

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}|v|^{2}f(v)dv

\displaystyle=\int_{|v|\leq 1}|v|^{2-q}dv+\int_{|v|>1}|v|^{2}e^{-|v|+1}dv

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{5-q}+260\pi<\infty.

Step 2:

Finite entropy.
Subsequently, we show that the entropy is finite.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}f|\log f|dv

\displaystyle=\int_{|v|\leq 1}|v|^{-q}\left|\log|v|^{-q}\right|dv+\int_{|v|>1}e^{-|v|+1}\left|-|v|+1\right|dv

\displaystyle=q\int_{|v|\leq 1}|v|^{-q}\log|v|^{-1}dv+\int_{|v|>1}(|v|-1)e^{-|v|+1}dv

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi q}{(3-q)^{2}}+44\pi<\infty.

Step 3:

Divergence of collision integral.
Finally, we verify that the following integral is divergent.

\displaystyle I=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v_{*}}}f(v)f(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}.

Since the integrand is non-negative, we can establish a lower bound for
I
by restricting the domain of integration to a subset
\Omega
where the singularity is most pronounced. We focus on the region near the origin where
|v|\leq 1
and
|v_{*}|\leq 1
.

\displaystyle I\geq\iint_{|v|\leq 1,|v_{*}|\leq 1}|v|^{-q}|v_{*}|^{-q}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dvdv_{*}.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be sufficiently small. We define the region
\Omega
as

\displaystyle\Omega=\left\{(v,v_{*})\in B(0,1)\times B(0,1)\;\middle|\;|v|\in(0,\epsilon),\ |v_{*}|\in\left(\frac{|v|}{2},|v|\right),\ \theta<\frac{\pi}{6}\right\},

where
\theta
is the angle between
v
and
v_{*}
.
Thus,

I\geq\iint_{\Omega}|v|^{-q}|v_{*}|^{-q}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}dv_{*}dv.

Next, we analyze the term
|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}
within
\Omega
. Using spherical coordinates
v=r\omega
,
v_{*}=r_{*}\omega_{*}
, the Law of Cosines yields

|v-v_{*}|^{2}=r^{2}+r_{*}^{2}-2rr_{*}\cos\theta.

Furthermore, in the region
\Omega
, we have
\frac{r}{2}<r_{*}<r
and
\cos\theta>\cos\frac{\pi}{6}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. In this region, we can derive an upper bound for the relative velocity:

\displaystyle|v-v_{*}|^{2}=(r-r_{*})^{2}+2rr_{*}(1-\cos\theta)\leq\left(r-\frac{r}{2}\right)^{2}+2r^{2}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right).

Specifically, there exists a constant
C_{0}
such that
|v-v_{*}|\leq C_{0}r.
Since the exponent
\gamma
is negative, we have

|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\geq C_{0}^{\gamma}r^{\gamma}.

Substituting the estimate into the integral
I
, we obtain

\displaystyle I\geq C_{0}^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2-q+\gamma}\left(\int_{r/2}^{r}\int_{\Omega_{\omega_{*}}}r_{*}^{2-q}d\omega_{*}dr_{*}\right)d\omega dr.

Here, the angular integration over the spherical cap defined by
\theta<\frac{\pi}{6}
yields a strictly positive constant, which we denote by
C_{1}
.
The inner radial integral with respect to
r_{*}
is evaluated as

\int_{r/2}^{r}r_{*}^{2-q}dr_{*}=\left[\frac{r_{*}^{3-q}}{3-q}\right]_{r/2}^{r}=\frac{1}{3-q}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{3-q}}\right)r^{3-q}.

Let
C^{\prime}=C_{0}^{\gamma}C_{1}(4\pi)^{2}\frac{1}{3-q}(1-2^{-(3-q)})
. Since
q<3
, it follows that
C^{\prime}
is a positive finite constant.
Finally, substituting these results back into the expression, we find that the integral
I
satisfies

\displaystyle I\geq C^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\epsilon}r^{2-q+\gamma}\cdot r^{3-q}dr=C^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\epsilon}r^{5-2q+\gamma}dr.

Recall that the integral
\int_{0}^{\epsilon}r^{k}\,dr
diverges if and only if
k\leq-1
. In the present case, the exponent is
5-2q+\gamma
. Under the condition
q>\frac{\gamma+6}{2}
, we have
5-2q+\gamma<-1
. Consequently, the integral
I
diverges.
∎
